0\to\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{m}\cap I}{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}\cap I}\cong\frac{(\mathfrak{n}^{m}\cap I)+\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}}{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}}\to\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{m}}{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}}\to\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{m}+I}{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}+I}\to 0

shows that
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{m}(f)=d^{m}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{m}\cap I}{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}\cap I}\right)
; compare
[
V3
, (4)]
. Thus the
m
th corank is determined by the conditions imposed on the leading terms of elements of the Jacobian ideal of order exactly
m
.
In particular,
0\leq\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{m}(f)\leq d^{m}
.
If
\ord(f)\geq m+1
, then

\frac{(\mathfrak{n}^{m}\cap I)+\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}}{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}}\cong\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}+I}{\mathfrak{n}^{m+1}}

matching (
1.A
), (
1.B
), and
4.4
.

By definition, the
\mathfrak{J}
-dimension is the growth of the sum of coranks.
Calculating the
m
th corank is not necessarily straightforward: essentially
it amounts to calculating a Gröbner basis of the Jacobian ideal with a local
monomial order to at least order
m
.

To study Jacobi algebras
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
of
\mathfrak{J}
-dimension
\leq 1
,

4.7
constrains
the number of variables to
d\leq 2
and
k=\ord(f)\leq 3
.
The corank controls the rank of
f_{2}
. The main case is when
f_{2}=0
,
when it is clear that
2\leq\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)\leq 4
.
The two derivatives
\updelta_{x}f_{3}
and
\updelta_{y}f_{3}
are linearly independent
when
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=2
and they are
dependent when
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=3
. The case
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=4
holds only when
f_{3}=0
,
which is ruled out by
4.7
.

This provides a numerical characterisation of the ADE types.
The first Type A case is when
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)=0
, in which case
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathbb{C}
.
In addition to this, if
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
has
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\leq 1
, we say
f
has Type A, D or E according to the following table.

Type

\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)

\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)

A

1

1

D

2

2

E

2

3

The higher coranks provide much more detail.
In Type A they provide enough information to classify up to isomorphism, however in Type D this is not true.

Example 4.11
.

Consider the families
D_{n,\infty}
and
D_{n,m}
from the introduction. The higher coranks are given by the following table.

Type

f

Conditions

\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{i}(f)
,
i=0,1,\dots

D_{n,\infty}

xy^{2}+x^{2n}

n\geq 2

1,\overbrace{2,\dots,2}^{2n-1},\overbrace{1,\dots,1}^{2n-2}

D_{n,m}

xy^{2}+x^{2n}+x^{2m+1}

2n-2\geq m\geq n\geq 2

D_{n,m}

xy^{2}+x^{2m+1}+x^{2n}

n>m\geq 1

1,\overbrace{2,\dots,2}^{2n-1},\overbrace{1,\dots,1}^{2m-1}

In particular
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=6n-3
in the first families, which is independent
of
m
, whilst
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=4n+2m-2
in the final family.

4.4.
Linear Changes in Coordinates and Discriminants

In light of
4.8
, from §
6
onwards we work in two non-commuting variables
x
and
y
.

The following is an immediate consequence of the Splitting Lemma and abelianisation.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with
f_{2}=ax^{2}+b_{1}xy+b_{2}yx+cy^{2}\not\sim 0
.
Set
b=b_{1}+b_{2}
and consider the discriminant
\Delta=b^{2}-4ac
.
Then
f\cong g
, for some
g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with

g_{2}=\begin{cases}x^{2}+y^{2}&\text{if }\Delta\neq 0\\
x^{2}&\text{if }\Delta=0.\end{cases}

As for the quadratic forms above, up to
\sim
we may commute variables appearing in
cubic forms in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
, and
we use this to simplify the statement of the following lemma, writing
bx^{2}y
rather than

b_{1}x^{2}y+b_{2}xyx+\dots
, and so on.
Note that, in general,
cyclic equivalence no longer simulates commutativity in higher degree, as
xyxy\nsim x^{2}y^{2}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

[
I
]

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
with

f_{3}\sim ax^{3}+bx^{2}y+cxy^{2}+dy^{3}
for some
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{C}
, not all zero.
Let
\Delta=-27a^{2}d^{2}+18abcd-4ac^{3}-4b^{3}d+b^{2}c^{2}\in\mathbb{C}
be the cubic discriminant.
Then

f\cong g
, for some
g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
with

g_{3}=\begin{cases}x^{3}+y^{3}&\text{if }\Delta\neq 0\\
x^{2}y&\text{if }\Delta=0\text{ and }\begin{cases}a(b^{2}-3ac)\neq 0\text{ or }\\
(c^{2}-3bd)d\neq 0\text{ or }\\
a=d=0\end{cases}\\
x^{3}&\text{otherwise}.\\
\end{cases}

Thus these three leading cubic normal forms are characterised by whether

f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has three, two or one distinct factors respectively.

Proof.

Consider the linear automorphism of
\mathbb{C}[x,y]

x\mapsto\upalpha x+\upbeta y,\qquad y\mapsto\upgamma x+\updelta y\qquad\text{for $\upalpha,\upbeta,\upgamma,\updelta\in\mathbb{C}$}

(4.B)

that maps
(f_{3})^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
to one of the normal forms
x^{3}+y^{3}
,
xy^{2}
or
x^{3}
.
The choice of normal form is determined by the cubic determinant. The
additional conditions on the coefficients in the statement are simply that
p\neq 0

in the depressed form after completing the cube

x^{3}+pxy^{2}+qy^{3}
, in which
\Delta=-4p^{3}-27q^{2}
,
and accounting for the fact that
a=0
or
d=0
or both are possible.

Let
\upvarphi
be the linear automorphism of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
defined by the same formula (
4.B
)
and
g_{3}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
be the corresponding cubic normal form.
Then
\upvarphi(f_{3})\sim g_{3}
, although they are not equal, so
\upvarphi(f)\sim g_{3}+\upvarphi(f_{\geq 4})
.
Thus
f\cong\upvarphi(f)\cong g_{3}+\upvarphi(f_{\geq 4})
, as claimed.
∎

5.
Type
A
and Commutativity

This section considers the most elementary situation, namely

f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with large quadratic part. Normal forms are established in §
5.1
. Together with the linear coordinate changes from §
4.4
, this proves in §
5.2
that for any
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
, the algebra
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is commutative if and only if
f
has corank at most
1
. This fact is used in later sections.

5.1.
Normal Forms of Type A

It is notationally convenient to identify
y=x_{d}
and work in the ring
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle=\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1},y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
.

Theorem 5.1
.

If
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)\leq 1
,
then there is a unique polynomial
g
of the form
g=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{d-1}^{2}+\upvarepsilon y^{n}
for some

n\geq 2
and
\upvarepsilon\in\{0,1\}
such that
f\cong g
.

(1)

If
\upvarepsilon=1
, then
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is commutative with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=n-1
.

(2)

If
\upvarepsilon=0
, then
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is commutative with
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=1
.

Proof.

By the Splitting Lemma
4.5
, there is
\mathsf{f}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1},y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
with
f\cong\mathsf{f}

and either

\mathsf{f}=\begin{cases}x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{d-1}^{2}+y^{2}&\text{if }\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)=0\\
x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{d-1}^{2}+q(y)&\text{if }\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)=1\end{cases}

for some
q\in\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]
with
\ord(q)\geq 3
.

If
q
is zero we are done, else after pulling out the lowest term, we can write
q=y^{n}\mathsf{u}
for some
\mathsf{u}=c_{n}+c_{n+1}y+\ldots\in\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]
with
c_{n}\neq 0
. The homomorphism
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1},y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\to\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1},y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
which sends
x_{k}\mapsto x_{k}
and
y\mapsto y\sqrt[n]{\mathsf{u}}
is an automorphism. Since
\sqrt[n]{\mathsf{u}}
is a power series only in
y
, it commutes with
y
, and so this automorphism sends
\sum x_{i}^{2}+y^{n}
to
\sum x_{i}^{2}+y^{n}\mathsf{u}=\mathsf{f}
. Hence
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\sum x_{i}^{2}+y^{n})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{f})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, as required.

Parts (1)–(2) are obvious, since
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\sum x_{i}^{2}+y^{n})\cong\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]/(y^{n-1})
and
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\sum x_{i}^{2})\cong\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]
, and uniqueness then follows since
\sum x_{i}^{2}+y^{n_{1}}\cong\sum x_{i}^{2}+y^{n_{2}}
if and only if
n_{1}=n_{2}
.
∎

Recall from the introduction our geometric applications in the setting of cDV singularities. These correspond to Jacobi algebras in two non-commutating
variables, so we set
d=2
and write the variables as
x,y
.

Corollary 5.2
.

Every
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, where
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with
f_{2}\not=0
, is geometric.

Proof.

Consider
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{C}[\![x,y,z]\!]^{\frac{1}{2}(1,1,0)}
, and its unique crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
. This has contraction algebra
\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2})
, realising the second case in
5.1
. On the other hand, by
[
DW1
, 3.10]
the Type
A

m
-Pagoda flop (with
m\geq 1
) has contraction algebra
\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]/y^{m}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2}+y^{m+1})
, which realises the infinite family in
5.1
.
∎

Example 5.3
.

Consider
f=x^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}(xy^{2}+yxy+y^{2}x)\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
.
This has
3
-dimensional Jacobi algebra

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/(\kern-2.5pt(x+y^{2},xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)\cong\mathbb{C}[y]/y^{3}

so, by
5.1
or Reid’s Pagoda
[
R2
]
,

f
gives the same Jacobi algebra as
g=x^{2}+y^{4}
. Commutatively,
one would see this by completing the square, but that automorphism does not work directly in
the noncommutative context:
x\mapsto x-\frac{2}{3}y^{2}
gives
f\mapsto x^{2}+\frac{2}{3}yxy-\frac{8}{9}y^{4}
, and we cannot attack the
yxy
term by coordinate changes that preserve
f_{2}=x^{2}
.
But
f\sim x^{2}+xy^{2}+y^{2}x
, which then allows us to complete the square (and a scalar on
y
) to conclude.
This exemplifies the way
\sim
helps to navigate the Jacobi isomorphism classes.

5.2.
Commutativity

The following characterisation of commutative Jacobi algebras in
d=2
variables will be used later.

Proposition 5.4
.

f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
, then
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is commutative if and only if
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)\leq 1
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) is clear from
5.1
. For (
\Rightarrow
), we prove the contrapositive. If
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)\not\leq 1
, then
f_{2}=0
, and we need to prove that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is not commutative. For this, it suffices to exhibit a factor that is not commutative. By
4.13
without loss of generality we can assume that
f
equals

x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4},\quad xy^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{4},\quad x^{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4}\quad\mbox{or}\quad\mathcal{O}_{4}.

Write
\mathcal{M}_{3}
for the set of all noncommutative monomials of degree
3
, then factor by the ideal
(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)/(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
. But in the four cases above, by differentiating then using the third isomorphism theorem it follows that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is one of

\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},y^{2},\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)},\quad\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(y^{2},xy+yx,\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)},\quad\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)},\quad\mbox{or}\quad\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)}.

None of these factors is commutative, and so
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is not commutative.
∎

6.
Type
D
normal forms

This section considers the next case, namely those
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)=2
and
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=2
. Reducing to two variables by the Splitting Lemma
4.5
, the assumption
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=2
is then equivalent to the first two cases in
4.13
, namely those
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
with
f_{3}\neq 0
for which
f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has either two or three distinct linear factors. Full normal forms are obtained in both situations, and are then merged into a unified form in §
6.4
. These are the Type
D
normal forms in the tables in §
1.2
.

Throughout this section, it will be convenient to adopt the following language.

Definition 6.1
.

We say that a monomial
m\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle

contains
x^{2}

if
m\sim nx^{2}
for some monomial
n
, else
m

does not contain
x^{2}
.
Similarly, an element
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
contains
x^{2}
if for some (nonzero) term
\uplambda m
of
f
, the monomial
m
contains
x^{2}
, else
f
does not contain
x^{2}
. We also use the analogous expressions for
y^{2}
.

6.1.
Abelianized Cubic with Three Factors

This subsection considers the case of
4.13

where
f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has three distinct factors, that is
f\cong x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4}
,
and in
6.5
and
6.7
provides two different, but equivalent, normal forms.

Recall from
2.2
(
1
) that a unitriangular automorphism
\upvarphi
of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
has depth
e\geq 1
if and only if
\upvarphi(f)_{\leq e}=f_{\leq e}
for all
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Fix
t\geq 4
, and let
f=x^{3}+y^{3}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t}+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}
. For any
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
with
\ord(h_{i})\geq t-2
, there is a unitriangular automorphism
\uppsi
of depth
\geq t-3
such that

\uppsi(f)\sim x^{3}+y^{3}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}+\left(f_{t}-h_{1}x^{2}-h_{2}y^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}

and
\uppsi(f)-(x^{3}+y^{3}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1})\in\mathfrak{n}^{t}
.

Proof.

Consider the unitriangular automorphism
\uppsi
which sends
x\mapsto x-\tfrac{1}{3}h_{1}
,
y\mapsto y-\tfrac{1}{3}h_{2}
. The result follows since

\uppsi(x^{3}+y^{3})\sim x^{3}-h_{1}x^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}h_{1}^{2}x-\tfrac{1}{27}h_{1}^{3}+y^{3}-h_{2}y^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}h_{2}^{2}y-\tfrac{1}{27}h_{2}^{3},

and
\uppsi(m)\equiv m\mod\mathfrak{n}^{t+1}
whenever
\deg(m)\geq 4
.
∎

With the preparatory lemma in place, the strategy is to first find a standard
power series form of each potential, and then distill that down to a polynomial normal form.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose that
f=f_{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4}
where
f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has three distinct factors. Then

f\cong x^{3}+y^{3}+p(xy)

for some power series
p(z)\in\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
with
\ord(p)\geq 2
.

Recall the Conventions
1.8
on denoting graded pieces of sequence
elements: we denote sequence elements
\mathsf{f}_{n}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
in Greek font,
and we write
(\mathsf{f}_{n})_{t}
for its
degree
t
piece, and
(\mathsf{f}_{n})_{<t}
and
(\mathsf{f}_{n})_{>t}

for sub- and super-degree
t
portions respectively.

Proof.

We construct a sequence of power series
\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\dots

and unitriangular automorphisms
\upvarphi^{1},\upvarphi^{2},\dots
inductively, with
each
\mathsf{f}_{t}
having the form of the target power series
x^{3}+y^{3}+p(xy)
in small degree. Summary
3.7
(
3
) then constructs
\mathsf{f}=\lim\mathsf{f}_{i}
of the required form with
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{f})
.

By
4.13
,
f\cong g
where
g_{3}=x^{3}+y^{3}
.
After grouping together terms containing
x^{2}
or
y^{2}
and cyclically permuting,
we may write

g\sim x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathsf{h}_{2}\cdot x^{2}+\mathsf{h}_{2}^{\prime}\cdot y^{2}+\upmu_{4}(xy)^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{5}

for
\mathsf{h}_{2},\mathsf{h}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{2}
and
\upmu_{4}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Hence we begin the induction by setting

\mathsf{f}_{1}=x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathsf{h}_{2}\cdot x^{2}+\mathsf{h}_{2}^{\prime}\cdot y^{2}+\upmu_{4}(xy)^{2}+g_{\geq 5}

and note that
\mathsf{f}_{1}\sim g\cong f
.
Thus
\mathsf{f}_{1}
is in the desired form in degrees
\leq 3
and has its
degree 4 piece prepared in standard form for further analysis.

For the inductive step more generally, we may suppose that
\mathsf{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle

has been constructed of the form

\mathsf{f}_{t}=\big(x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathsf{p}_{t+2}(xy)\big)+\big(\mathsf{h}_{t+1}\cdot x^{2}+\mathsf{h}_{t+1}^{\prime}\cdot y^{2}+\upmu_{t+3}(xy)^{\lfloor(t+3)/2\rfloor}\big)+\mathcal{O}_{t+4}

with
\mathsf{p}_{3}=0
and by convention
\upmu_{t+3}=0
for even
t
, where

(1)

(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{\leq t+2}=x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathsf{p}_{t+2}(xy)
, for some polynomial
\mathsf{p}_{t+2}\in\mathbb{C}[z]_{\geq 2}
of degree
\leq(t+2)/2
, where the polynomials
\mathsf{p}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{p}_{t+2}
satisfy
\mathsf{p}_{i+1}=\mathsf{p}_{i}
for even
i
and

\mathsf{p}_{i+1}-\mathsf{p}_{i}=\upmu_{i+1}z^{(i+1)/2}

for odd
i
, and

(2)

(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{t+3}=\mathsf{h}_{t+1}\cdot x^{2}+\mathsf{h}_{t+1}^{\prime}\cdot y^{2}+\upmu_{t+3}(xy)^{\lfloor(t+3)/2\rfloor}
for some homogeneous forms
\mathsf{h}_{t+1}
,
\mathsf{h}_{t+1}^{\prime}
of degree
t+1
.

Applying
6.2
with
h_{1}=\mathsf{h}_{t+1}
and
h_{2}=\mathsf{h}_{t+1}^{\prime}
, there exists a unitriangular
\upvarphi^{t}
of depth
\geq t
such that

\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})\sim\big(x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathsf{p}_{t+2}(xy)\big)+\upmu_{t+3}(xy)^{\lfloor(t+3)/2\rfloor}+\mathcal{O}_{t+4}.

In degree
t+4
, again grouping together the terms containing
x^{2}
or
y^{2}
and cyclically permuting, we may write

\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{t+4}\sim\mathsf{h}_{t+2}\cdot x^{2}+\mathsf{h}_{t+2}^{\prime}\cdot y^{2}+\upmu_{t+4}(xy)^{\lfloor(t+4)/2\rfloor}

for homogeneous forms
\mathsf{h}_{t+2}
,
\mathsf{h}_{t+2}^{\prime}
of degree
t+2
and some
\upmu_{t+4}\in\mathbb{C}
, where again
\upmu_{t+4}=0
for odd
t
. Thus, after setting
\mathsf{p}_{t+3}(xy)=\mathsf{p}_{t+2}(xy)+\upmu_{t+3}(xy)^{\lfloor(t+3)/2\rfloor}
, define

\mathsf{f}_{t+1}=x^{3}+y^{3}+\mathsf{p}_{t+3}(xy)+\big(\mathsf{h}_{t+2}\cdot x^{2}+\mathsf{h}_{t+2}^{\prime}\cdot y^{2}+\upmu_{t+4}(xy)^{\lfloor(t+4)/2\rfloor}\big)+\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{\geq t+5}.

Note that
\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})\sim\mathsf{f}_{t+1}
, and
\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})-\mathsf{f}_{t+1}\in\mathfrak{n}^{t+3}\subset\mathfrak{n}^{t+1}
using the last statement of
6.2
.

Thus we have constructed a sequence of power series
\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\dots
and unitriangular
automorphisms
\upvarphi^{1},\upvarphi^{2},\dots
to which
3.7
(
3
) applies.
For
s\geq 3
either
s
is even, in which case
\mathsf{p}_{s+1}=\mathsf{p}_{s}
, or
s
is odd, in which case
\mathsf{p}_{s+1}=\mathsf{p}_{s}+\upmu_{s+1}z^{(s+1)/2}
, thus it is clear that

p\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim\mathsf{p}_{s}=\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\upmu_{2s}z^{s}
. Further,
\mathsf{f}=\lim\mathsf{f}_{i}=x^{3}+y^{3}+p(xy)
, since the difference

(\mathsf{f}_{i}-(x^{3}+y^{3}+p(xy)))_{i\geq 1}
converges to zero.

It follows from
3.7
(
3
) that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{3}+y^{3}+p(xy))
, as required.
∎

The next step is to replace the power series
p(xy)
by its leading term, without changing the Jacobi algebra.

Lemma 6.4
.

If
f=x^{3}+y^{3}+p(xy)\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
for some
0\neq p(z)\in\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
for which
s=\ord(p)\geq 2
, then the following statements hold.

(1)

yx^{2},xy^{2}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
.

(2)

x^{2}y,y^{2}x\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
.

(3)

(xy)^{s}x,(yx)^{s}y\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
.

Proof.

(1) Write
J_{f}=(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)
, so that
(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
is the closure of
J_{f}
. Differentiating and pulling out the lowest terms, write

\updelta_{x}f=3x^{2}+y(xy)^{s-1}q(xy)\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\updelta_{y}f=3y^{2}+q(xy)(xy)^{s-1}x

(6.A)

for some
q=\uplambda_{0}+\uplambda_{1}z+\uplambda_{2}z^{2}+\cdots\in\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
with
\uplambda_{0}\neq 0
.
Writing
A\equiv B
for
A-B\in J_{f}
, then in particular

x^{2}\equiv-\tfrac{1}{3}y(xy)^{s-1}q(xy)\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad y^{2}\equiv-\tfrac{1}{3}q(xy)(xy)^{s-1}x.

(6.B)

Substituting for
x^{2}
or
y^{2}
at each step, we see that

\displaystyle y\cdot x^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{-1}{3}y^{2}\cdot(xy)^{s-1}q(xy)

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{n}^{2s\phantom{+2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{+1}{3^{2}}q(xy)(xy)^{s-1}x\cdot(xy)^{s-1}q(xy)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{+1}{3^{2}}q(xy)(xy)^{s-1}\cdot x^{2}\cdot y(xy)^{s-2}q(xy)

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{n}^{4s-3}

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{-1}{3^{3}}q(xy)(xy)^{s-1}\cdot y(xy)^{s-1}q(xy)\cdot y(xy)^{s-2}q(xy)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{-1}{3^{3}}q(xy)(xy)^{s-2}x\cdot y^{2}\cdot(xy)^{s-1}q(xy)y(xy)^{s-2}q(xy)

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{n}^{6s-6}

\displaystyle=\ldots

At each substitution, the resulting power series has order
2s-3\geq 1
higher than the previous one. It follows from the above that for all
t\geq 2s
there exists
n_{t}\in\mathfrak{n}^{t}
such that
yx^{2}-n_{t}\in J_{f}
. Hence
yx^{2}\in\bigcap_{t\geq 2s}(J_{f}+\mathfrak{n}^{t})
, which is precisely the closure
(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
. By symmetry in
x
and
y
, the analogous statement
xy^{2}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
also follows.

(2) This follows in an analogous way: start by writing

\updelta_{x}f=3x^{2}+r(yx)(yx)^{s-1}y\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\updelta_{y}f=3y^{2}+x(yx)^{s-1}r(yx)

then consider
x^{2}\cdot y\equiv\tfrac{-1}{3}r(yx)(yx)^{s-1}y^{2}
, etc.

(3) Now write
A\equiv B
for
A-B\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
. Separating off the lowest term of
q(xy)
in (
6.A
), we may write

\updelta_{y}f=3y^{2}+\uplambda_{0}(xy)^{s-1}x+(q(xy)-\uplambda_{0})(xy)^{s-1}x

and so
\uplambda_{0}(xy)^{s-1}x\equiv-3y^{2}-(q(xy)-\uplambda_{0})(xy)^{s-1}x
where
q(xy)-\uplambda_{0}\in\mathfrak{n}^{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle\uplambda_{0}(xy)^{s}x

\displaystyle=\left(\uplambda_{0}(xy)^{s-1}x\right)yx

\displaystyle\equiv\left(-3y^{2}-(q(xy)-\uplambda_{0})(xy)^{s-1}x\right)(yx)

\displaystyle=-3y\cdot y^{2}\cdot x-(q(xy)-\uplambda_{0})(xy)^{s}x

\displaystyle\equiv y\cdot q(xy)(xy)^{s-1}x\cdot x-(q(xy)-\uplambda_{0})(xy)^{s}x

(by (
6.B
))

\displaystyle\equiv-(q(xy)-\uplambda_{0})(xy)^{s}x

(
yx^{2}\equiv 0
by (
1
))

The
\uplambda_{0}(xy)^{s}x
on each side cancel, showing that
q(xy)(xy)^{s}x\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
. Since
q(xy)
is a unit, it follows that
(xy)^{s}x\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
. Again, appealing to symmetry in
x
and
y
proves the final statement.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose that
f=x^{3}+y^{3}+p(xy)
where
p(z)\in\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
with
s=\ord(p)\geq 2
.
Then

f\cong\begin{cases}x^{3}+y^{3}&\text{when $p=0$}\\
x^{3}+y^{3}+(xy)^{s}&\text{when $p\neq 0$}.\\
\end{cases}

Furthermore

(1)

\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\leq 1
, with equality if and only if
p=0
.

(2)

If
p\neq 0
, then
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=4s
, and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}=4
.

Therefore the expressions
x^{3}+y^{3}+(xy)^{s}
with
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\cup\{\infty\}
form a set of normal forms.

Proof.

For the first statement, if
p=0
we are done, so suppose
p\neq 0
. Continuing the notation in the proof of
6.4
above, after differentiating and pulling out the lowest terms, we may write

\updelta_{x}f=3x^{2}+y(xy)^{s-1}q(xy)\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\updelta_{y}f=3y^{2}+q(xy)(xy)^{s-1}x

(6.C)

for some
q=\uplambda_{0}+\uplambda_{1}z+\uplambda_{2}z^{2}+\cdots\in\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
with
\uplambda_{0}\neq 0
. Set
g=x^{3}+y^{3}+(\uplambda_{0}/s)(xy)^{s}
. Now
6.4
applies equally well to both
f
and
g
, hence both
(xy)^{s}x
and
(yx)^{s}y
belong to
both
the Jacobi ideals associated to
f
and
g
. Consequently

\displaystyle(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)

\displaystyle=(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,(xy)^{s}x,(yx)^{s}y)\kern-2.5pt)

\displaystyle=(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}g,\updelta_{y}g,(xy)^{s}x,(yx)^{s}y)\kern-2.5pt)

(cancel higher terms from
\updelta_{x}f
and
\updelta_{y}f
)

\displaystyle=(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}g,\updelta_{y}g)\kern-2.5pt).

It follows that
f\simeq g
. The coordinate change
x\mapsto ax
,
y\mapsto ay
for
a=\sqrt[2s-3]{s/\uplambda_{0}}
then normalises the constant factor
\uplambda_{0}/s\neq 0
, as required.

(
1
) Consider the case
p=0
. As in
3.2
, if
\mathfrak{J}
is the Jacobson radical of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, then

\frac{\mathfrak{J}^{d}}{\mathfrak{J}^{d+1}}=\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{d}+(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},y^{2})\kern-2.5pt)}{\mathfrak{n}^{d+1}+(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},y^{2})\kern-2.5pt)}.

This is always a two-dimensional vector space, since if
d
is even it has basis
(xy)^{d/2}
and
(yx)^{d/2}
, whilst if
d
is odd it has basis
x(yx)^{(d-1)/2}
and
y(xy)^{(d-1)/2}
. It follows that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is an infinite-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, with
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=1
.

When
p\neq 0
,
6.4
shows at once that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite dimensional:
indeed any monomial of degree
t\geq 2s+1
either contains one of the monomials listed in
6.4
(
1
–
3
) or is
x^{t}
or
y^{t}
. But by (
6.C
)
x^{t}=x^{t-4}\cdot x^{2}\cdot x^{2}
and
y^{t}=y^{2}\cdot y^{2}\cdot y^{t-4}

is equivalent, modulo
(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
, to a monomial that contains one of those listed, and thus is equivalent to zero. Consequently, the entire graded piece of degree
t
is zero, and so
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite dimensional.

(
2
) We compute a standard basis of
(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
with respect to a local graded monomial order, where we refer the reader to
6.6
below for references to the formal theory and its properties in this case. In particular, leading terms have lowest degree, and lexicographical order selects the leading term when there is more than one of lowest degree.

The proof of (
1
) above introduces a simplifying factor:
since all monomials of degree
t=2s+1
lie in the closed Jacobian ideal, it is sufficient
to work in the quotient

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle x,y\rangle}{(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,\mathcal{M}_{t})}

where we write
\mathcal{M}_{t}
for the set of all noncommutative monomials of degree
t
,
and closure is no longer an issue.

Following
[
GH
, 3.6]
,
we compile a standard basis
\{g_{1},g_{2},\dots\}
of
(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,\mathcal{M}^{t})

starting with the normalised derivatives

g_{1}=x^{2}+\tfrac{s}{3}y(xy)^{s-1}=\tfrac{1}{3}\updelta_{x}f\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}=y^{2}+\tfrac{s}{3}x(yx)^{s-1}=\tfrac{1}{3}\updelta_{y}f

which have leading terms
x^{2}
and
y^{2}
respectively, since
s\geq 2
.
The computation proceeds by resolving non-trivial overlaps among leading terms.
The leading term of
g_{1}
overlaps non-trivially with itself to produce
(after scaling by
\tfrac{3}{s}
to normalise coefficients)

g_{3}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\tfrac{3}{s}(xg_{1}-g_{1}x)=(xy)^{s}-(yx)^{s}.

This does not reduce further modulo existing leading terms.
The analogous overlap
yg_{2}-g_{2}y
gives the same
g_{3}
,
and all other overlaps have order
\geq t
, so reduce to zero modulo
\mathcal{M}_{t}
.
Hence the standard basis is
\{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\}
.
Note that we can then dispense with
\mathcal{M}_{t}
, since those
monomials all reduce to zero under
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
– that is exactly what
6.4
demonstrates – and so they do not appear in the standard basis.

As in the commutative case, the set of monomials not divisible by the leading term of any of
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
,
g_{3}
descends to give a monomial
\mathbb{C}
-vector space basis for the quotient, by e.g.
[
GH
, 3.5 and 3.1–2]
.
Thus, working in increasing degree,
1,x,y
are in the basis. Then,
in each pair of degrees
2e,2e+1
for
1\leq e\leq s-1
the basis consists of the four monomials

(xy)^{e},(yx)^{e},\ (xy)^{e}x,(yx)^{e}y,

and finally
(xy)^{s}\equiv(yx)^{s}
in degree
2s
.
Summing up, this basis has size
4s
, as claimed.

In the abelianisation, if
s>2
we may rewrite the derivatives as
x^{2}(\mathrm{unit})
and
y^{2}(\mathrm{unit})
. Hence
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}\cong\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]/(x^{2},y^{2})
, which is four dimensional. When
s=2
, it is also easy to verify that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}\cong\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]/(x^{2},y^{2})
, so in all cases the dimension is four.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

The theory of standard bases, also known as local Gröbner bases, of ideals and their closures in noncommutative power series rings is less well documented in the literature than either global polynomial Gröbner bases (commutative or not), or Mora’s tangent cone algorithm for commutative power series rings; see for example
[
M3
]

or
[
H
, III.1]
.
Nevertheless, the theory exists following analogous ideas, and has analogous
conclusions.
The essential reference is
[
GH
]
, where §3 establishes the existence and properties of
standard bases, whilst §4–5 provides the tools needed to calculate.
Standard bases may be infinite in general, but within the context
of
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\leq 1
examples in this paper, this issue does not arise.

In order to state a unified theorem with the
xy^{2}
case
in §
6.4
below, it is convenient to mildly change basis.
This is rather cheap, largely because there are no moduli.

Corollary 6.7
.

Suppose that
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
where
f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has three roots. Then either

f\cong\begin{cases}xy^{2}+x^{3}&\\
xy^{2}+x^{3}+x^{2n}&\mbox{for some }n\geq 2.\\
\end{cases}

Furthermore, the above are normal forms.

Proof.

Each of the forms
f
listed satisfies the condition that
f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has three roots. Thus there exists some
g
from the list in
6.5
with
g\cong f
. Furthermore,
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{3})=\infty
, whereas
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{3}+x^{2n})=4n
(see e.g.
[
vG
, §5]
and
[
Ka
, §5]
, or
4.11
), and so all options are uniquely covered. Since the
g
listed in
6.5
are normal forms, it follows that the
f
listed here are normal forms.
∎

6.2.
Isomorphisms on the Quantum Plane

The following, which may be of independent interest, is one of the key reduction steps that will be used in §
6.3
.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any units
v,w\in\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
, the unitriangular automorphism
\upvarphi
of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
sending
x\mapsto xv
,
y\mapsto yw
descends to a topological isomorphism

\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\cong}}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)}.

Proof.

The inverse of
\upvarphi
, as an automorphism of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
, is clearly given by the unitriangular automorphism
\uppsi\colon x\mapsto xv^{-1}
,
y\mapsto yw^{-1}
. Set
I=(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)
, then since
\upvarphi
is a topological isomorphism by
2.3
, we just need to prove that
\upvarphi(I)=I
.

Now
x
commutes with
v
and
w
, being power series in
x^{2}
, and also
vw=wv
. But, modulo
I=(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)
,
y
commutes with
x^{2}
, thus since the ideal is closed
y
commutes with both
v
and
w
. It follows that

\upvarphi(xy+yx)=xvyw+ywxv\equiv(xy+yx)vw\equiv 0\quad\textnormal{mod }I.

(6.D)

Since
\upvarphi
is a continuous isomorphism, and
I
is the smallest closed ideal containing
xy+yx
,
\upvarphi(I)
is the smallest closed ideal containing
\upvarphi(xy+yx)
. But by (
6.D
)
\upvarphi(xy+yx)
also belongs to the closed ideal
I
, so by minimality
\upvarphi(I)\subseteq I
.

Since
v^{-1}
and
w^{-1}
are also units in
\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
, exactly the same logic applied to
\uppsi
shows that
\uppsi(I)\subseteq I
. Applying
\upvarphi
to this inclusion, we see that
I=\upvarphi\uppsi(I)\subseteq\upvarphi(I)
. Combining inclusions gives
\upvarphi(I)=I
.
∎

6.3.
Abelianized Cubic with Two Factors

This subsection considers the case of
4.13

where
f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has two distinct factors, that is
f\cong x^{2}y+\mathcal{O}_{4}
, and in
6.18
provides normal forms.
This is substantially harder than in §
6.1
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Fix
t\geq 4
, and let
f=xy^{2}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t}+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}
. For any
h\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
with
\ord(h)=t-2
, the unitriangular automorphism
x\mapsto x-h
,
y\mapsto y
sends

f\mapsto xy^{2}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}+\left(f_{t}-hy^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}.

Proof.

Write
\uppsi
for the stated automorphism. The result follows since
\uppsi(xy^{2})=xy^{2}-hy^{2}
and
\uppsi(m)\equiv m\mod\mathfrak{n}^{t+1}
whenever
\deg(m)\geq 4
.
∎

The next lemma is much less elementary.

Lemma 6.10
.

Fix
t\geq 4
, and let
f=xy^{2}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t}+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}
, where furthermore

f_{t}=\mathsf{h}_{t-2}\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upalpha x^{t}

(6.E)

for some homogeneous form
\mathsf{h}_{t-2}
of degree
t-2
,
each
a_{i}\geq 1
,
r\geq 1
and
r+\sum a_{i}=t
,
\upalpha\in\mathbb{C}
and each
\uplambda_{a}=\uplambda_{a_{1}\cdots a_{r}}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Then there exists a unitriangular automorphism
\upvarphi
of depth
\geq t-3
such that

\upvarphi(f)=xy^{2}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}+(g_{t}+\upalpha x^{t})+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}.

where
g_{t}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{t}
satisfies
g_{t}\sim 0
.

Example 6.11
.

It is worth considering an example to make the notation of both the statement and proof more transparent. Consider

f=xy^{2}+\Big(\uplambda_{51}x^{5}yxy+\uplambda_{42}x^{4}yx^{2}y+\uplambda_{33}x^{3}yx^{3}y\Big)+f_{\geq 9}

which has
f_{8}
of the form (
6.E
).
Applying
\upvarphi_{1}\colon x\mapsto x
and
y\mapsto y-\uplambda_{33}x^{3}yx^{2}
, where we cancelled
xy
from the right of the target
\uplambda_{33}
term to obtain the subtracted term, gives

\upvarphi_{1}(f)=xy^{2}+\uplambda_{51}x^{5}yxy+(\uplambda_{42}-\uplambda_{33})x^{4}yx^{2}y+g_{1}+\mathcal{O}_{9}

where
g_{1}=\uplambda_{33}(x^{3}yx^{3}y-xyx^{3}yx^{2})\sim 0
.
Ignoring
g_{1}
, the summation in degree 8 symbolically now has only two terms, which is
progress.

An analogous automorphism
\upvarphi_{2}
sending
x\mapsto x
and
y\mapsto-(\uplambda_{42}-\uplambda_{33})x^{4}yx
, where we cancelled
xy
from the right of the next target term, gives

\upvarphi_{2}\upvarphi_{1}(f)=xy^{2}+(\uplambda_{51}-\uplambda_{42}+\uplambda_{33})x^{5}yxy+g_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{9}

for some
g_{2}\sim 0
, and again the number of terms in degree 8 (outside
g_{2}
)
has not increased.
Repeating again with an analogous automorphism
\upvarphi_{3}
gives

\upvarphi_{3}\upvarphi_{2}\upvarphi_{1}(f)=xy^{2}-(\uplambda_{51}-\uplambda_{42}+\uplambda_{33})x^{6}y^{2}+g_{3}+\mathcal{O}_{9}

with
g_{3}\sim 0
.
We are now in a position to apply
6.9
to leave only
g_{3}
in degree 8.

The proof below confirms that this inductive idea works more generally.

Proof.

If the middle sum in the expression for
f_{t}
is zero, we are done by
6.9
(with
g_{t}=0
), so we may assume that the sum is nonzero.

Suppose that the middle sum contains a term
\mathsf{t}_{1}=\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}}y\cdots x^{b_{r}}y
with
r>1
.
In this case, consider the unitriangular automorphism
\upvarphi
defined by
x\mapsto x
,
y\mapsto y-\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1}
, where we have simply cancelled
xy
from the right-hand side of the target term
\mathsf{t}_{1}
. As in
6.9
,
\upvarphi(m)\equiv m\mod\mathfrak{n}^{t+1}
whenever
\deg(m)\geq 4
, so any change in degree
\leq t
comes from
\upvarphi(xy^{2})
, and thus

\displaystyle\upvarphi(f)=xy^{2}

\displaystyle+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}

\displaystyle+(f_{t}-\uplambda_{b}xyx^{b_{1}}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1}-\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}+1}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1}y)+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}

(6.F)

Writing

\mathsf{g}_{1}=\mathsf{t}_{1}-\uplambda_{b}xyx^{b_{1}}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1}\sim 0
,
then
the degree
t
term of (
6.F
) equals

\mathsf{g}_{1}+h\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upalpha x^{t}

where, under the summand, the target term
\mathsf{t}_{1}

has been
replaced by a term of the form
\mathsf{t}_{2}=-\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}+1}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1}y
, so the sum has the same
number of terms or fewer (depending on whether
\mathsf{t}_{2}
cancels with existing terms or not).

If
b_{r}=1
, then the new term
\mathsf{t}_{2}

equals
hy^{2}
for
h=-\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}+1}y\cdots yx^{b_{r-1}}
, and
we so may apply
6.9
to (
6.F
) to find
\uppsi
such that

\uppsi\upvarphi(f)=xy^{2}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}+(f_{t}-\uplambda_{b}xyx^{b_{1}}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1})+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}

where the degree
t
term is equal to

\displaystyle f_{t}-\uplambda_{b}xyx^{b_{1}}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}_{1}+f_{t}-\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}}y\cdots x^{b_{r}}y

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}_{1}+h\cdot y^{2}+\sum_{a\neq b}\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upalpha x^{t}

and the number of terms under the summand is now strictly reduced.

Otherwise,
b_{r}>1
. Set
\upvarphi_{1}=\upvarphi
, and repeating the original construction of a unitriangular automorphism by cancelling
xy
from the right, we can construct
\upvarphi_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{2}\upvarphi_{1}(f)=xy^{2}

\displaystyle+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}

\displaystyle+\big(\mathsf{g}_{2}+h\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upalpha x^{t}\big)+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}

(6.G)

where
\mathsf{g}_{2}\sim 0
is the sum of
\mathsf{g}_{1}
and another binomial
\sim 0
, and in the sum we have replaced the term
-\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}+1}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-1}y
by
\uplambda_{b}x^{b_{1}+2}y\cdots yx^{b_{r}-2}y
. Repeating this, we find unitriangular automorphisms
\upvarphi_{1},\dots,\upvarphi_{b_{r}-1}
so that
\upvarphi_{b_{r}-1}\cdots\upvarphi_{2}\upvarphi_{1}(f)
has the form of (
6.G
), and
the sum has the same number of terms or fewer, but in which the target
monomial we are focussing on
has become
x^{b_{1}+b_{r}-1}yx^{b_{2}}\cdots yxy
. A further repetition with a unitriangular
automorphism
\upvarphi_{b_{r}}
replaces that term by one that contains
y^{2}
, and once
again we may apply
6.9
to find a unitriangular automorphism
\uppsi
that moves this term into higher degree.
Thus after applying the single unitriangular automorphism
\uppsi\upvarphi_{b_{r}}\cdots\upvarphi_{1}

to
f
, the number of terms in the summation when parsed in the form (
6.E
) has
strictly reduced.

We repeat this process inductively, and it will terminate when there are no terms
under the summation sign of the form
\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y
with
r>1
.
Each step was achieved by a single unitriangular automorphism (itself built as a composition
of unitriangular automorphisms), and composing each of these gives a single
unitriangular automorphism
\upvartheta
such that

\upvartheta(f)=xy^{2}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}+(\mathsf{g}+h\cdot y^{2}+\uplambda x^{t-1}y+\upalpha x^{t})+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}

for some
\mathsf{g}
with
\mathsf{g}\sim 0
.

To conclude, the unitriangular automorphism
\upvarphi
defined by
x\mapsto x-h
,
y\mapsto y-\frac{\uplambda}{2}x^{a_{1}-1}=y-\frac{\uplambda}{2}x^{t-2}
has depth
t-3
, so again
\upvarphi(m)\equiv m\mod\mathfrak{n}^{t+1}
whenever
\deg(m)\geq 4
and thus

\upvarphi\upvartheta(f)=xy^{2}+f_{4}+\cdots+f_{t-1}+(\mathsf{g}+\underbrace{\uplambda x^{t-1}y-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}xyx^{t-2}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}x^{t-1}y}_{\mathsf{h}}+\upalpha x^{t})+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}.

Set
g_{t}=\mathsf{g}+\mathsf{h}
, then since both
\mathsf{g}\sim 0
and
\mathsf{h}\sim 0
, we are done.
∎

From here, the strategy of §
6.1
remains: first find a standard power series form of each potential, then simplify into polynomial normal form.

Proposition 6.12
.

Suppose that
f=f_{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4}
where
f_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has two distinct linear factors. Then

f\cong xy^{2}+q(x)
for some power series
q(x)\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]

with
\ord(q)\geq 4
.

Recall the Conventions
1.8
, used in
6.3
, on graded pieces of sequence elements: namely sequence elements
\mathsf{f}_{n}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
are in Greek font,
whilst
(\mathsf{f}_{n})_{t}
is the degree
t
piece of
\mathsf{f}_{n}
.

Proof.

We construct a sequence of power series
\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\dots
and unitriangular
automorphisms
\upvarphi^{1},\upvarphi^{2},\dots
inductively, with
each
\mathsf{f}_{t}
having the form of the target power series
xy^{2}+q(x)
in low degree.
Summary
3.7
(
3
) will then construct
\mathsf{f}=\lim\mathsf{f}_{i}
of the required form with
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{f})
.

By
4.13
,
f\cong g
where
g_{3}=xy^{2}
. After grouping together the terms containing
y^{2}
, then the terms that contain
y
but not
y^{2}
, and cyclically permuting, we may write

g_{4}\sim\mathsf{h}_{2}\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upmu_{4}x^{4}

for
\mathsf{h}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\langle x,y\rangle_{2}
,
r\geq 1
and each
a_{i}\geq 1
and
\upmu_{4}\in\mathbb{C}

and where we use the abbreviated notation
\uplambda_{a}:=\uplambda_{a_{1}\cdots a_{r}}\in\mathbb{C}
. It is convenient to write the sum as
\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y

by analogy with the general case, noting that here it is nothing more than

\uplambda_{11}xyxy+\uplambda_{3}x^{3}y
.

Hence we begin the induction by setting

\mathsf{f}_{1}=xy^{2}+(\mathsf{h}_{2}\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upmu_{4}x^{4})+g_{\geq 5}

and note that
\mathsf{f}_{1}\sim g\cong f
. Thus
\mathsf{f}_{1}
is in the desired form in degrees
\leq 3
and has its degree
4
piece prepared in standard form for further analysis.

For the inductive step more generally, we may suppose that
\mathsf{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
has been constructed of the form

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{t}=\big(xy^{2}+\mathsf{q}_{t+2}(x)\big)+\big({\mathsf{h}}_{t+1}\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upmu_{t+3}x^{t+3}\big)+\mathcal{O}_{t+4}

with
\mathsf{q}_{3}=0
and

(1)

(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{\leq t+2}=xy^{2}+\mathsf{q}_{t+2}(x)
, for some polynomial
\mathsf{q}_{t+2}\in\mathbb{C}[x]_{\geq 4}
of degree
\leq t+2
, where the polynomials
\mathsf{q}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{q}_{t+2}
satisfy
\mathsf{q}_{i+1}-\mathsf{q}_{i}=\upmu_{i+1}x^{i+1}
for
\upmu_{i+1}\in\mathbb{C}
, and

(2)

(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{t+3}=\mathsf{h}_{t+1}\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upmu_{t+3}x^{t+3}
for some homogeneous form
\mathsf{h}_{t+1}
of degree
t+1
,
each
a_{i}\geq 1
,
r\geq 1
and
r+\sum a_{i}=t+3
, and
\upmu_{t+3}\in\mathbb{C}
.

By
6.10
there exists a unitriangular
\upvarphi^{t}
of depth
t
such that

\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})=xy^{2}+q_{t+2}(x)+(k_{t+3}+\upmu_{t+3}x^{t+3})+\mathcal{O}_{t+4}.

where
k_{t+3}\sim 0
. In degree
t+4
, again grouping together the terms containing
y^{2}
, then the terms that contain
y
but not
y^{2}
, and cyclically permuting, we may write

\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{t+4}\sim\mathsf{h}_{t+2}\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upmu_{t+4}x^{t+4}.

Thus after setting
\mathsf{q}_{t+3}(x)=\mathsf{q}_{t+2}(x)+\upmu_{t+3}x^{t+3}
, define

\mathsf{f}_{t+1}=xy^{2}+\mathsf{q}_{t+3}(x)+\big(\mathsf{h}_{t+2}\cdot y^{2}+\sum\uplambda_{a}x^{a_{1}}y\ldots x^{a_{r}}y+\upmu_{t+4}x^{t+4}\big)+\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})_{\geq t+5}.

Note that
\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})\sim\mathsf{f}_{t+1}
, and that
\upvarphi^{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t})-\mathsf{f}_{t+1}\in\mathfrak{n}^{t+3}\subset\mathfrak{n}^{t+1}
.

Thus we have constructed a sequence of power series
\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\dots
and unitriangular
automorphisms
\upvarphi^{1},\upvarphi^{2},\dots
to which
3.7
(
3
) applies.
Since at each stage
\mathsf{q}_{s}=\mathsf{q}_{s-1}+\upmu_{s}x^{s}
, it is clear that

q\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim\mathsf{q}_{s}=\sum_{s=4}^{\infty}\upmu_{s}x^{s}
,
and that
\mathsf{f}=\lim\mathsf{f}_{i}=xy^{2}+q
, since the difference

(\mathsf{f}_{i}-(xy^{2}+q))_{i\geq 1}
converges to zero.
Hence
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+q)
, as required.
∎

The next step is to reduce the options for
q(x)
, using the following preliminary lemma.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]
be an even power series: that is,
u
is a power series in
x^{2}
.

(1)

If
u
is a unit, then
u^{-1}
and
\sqrt[n]{u}
are also even power series
for any
n\geq 2
.

(2)

Let
U\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]
be a unit and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
a nonzero integer.
Then there is a unit
t\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]
with
t^{n}=U(xt)
.
Furthermore, if
U
is even then
t
is even.

Proof.

(1) Consider
v\in\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
with
u(x)=v(x^{2})
. If
u
is a unit, then
v
is a unit
and
v^{-1}
and
\sqrt[n]{v}\in\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
for all
n\geq 2
.
Then
u^{-1}(x)=v^{-1}(x^{2})
and
\sqrt[n]{u}(x)=\sqrt[n]{v}(x^{2})
.

(2) Write
U=a_{0}+a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2}+\cdots
with
a_{0}\not=0
. Consider the case
n>0
.
We show that we may solve inductively for the coefficients
b_{d}
of the expansion
t=b_{0}+b_{1}x+b_{2}x^{2}+\cdots
in the equation
t^{n}=U(xt)
.

It is clear that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
t^{n}
is a sum of
nb_{0}^{n-1}b_{d}
with terms involving only coefficients
b_{i}
with
i<d
.
On the other side of the equation, the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
U(xt)
is a sum of terms involving
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
with
i\leq d
and
j<d
.
Putting these together,
b_{d}
does not appear in the coefficient of
x^{i}
for any
i<d
,
and it appears linearly with nonzero coefficient for the first time in the coefficient of
x^{d}
, and so we may solve for it. Working inductively in increasing
d\geq 0
,
and taking the limit, determines
t
as claimed.
For
n<0
, the same argument proves the existence of the unit
t^{-1}
,
which is equivalent.

Suppose that
U
is even, and let
b_{2n+1}
be the smallest nonzero odd-degree coefficient of
t
.
Then the odd-degree term with smallest degree in
t^{n}
is

nb_{0}^{n-1}b_{2n+1}x^{2n+1}
, while in
U(xt)
it is
2a_{2}b_{0}b_{2n+1}x^{2n+3}

which appears in the summand
a_{2}(xt)^{2}
, a contradiction.
So
t
must be even.
∎

The point is now simple:
y
is under control, and so there is a relation
xy+yx
in the Jacobi algebra. Then
6.8
yields the following key preparation result.

Proposition 6.14
.

If
f=xy^{2}+p(x)
where
p(x)\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]
with
\ord(p)\geq 4
, then

f\cong xy^{2}+\upalpha\,x^{2n}+\upbeta\,x^{2m+1}

for some
n,m\geq 2
, and some
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\{0,1\}
.
Furthermore,
\upalpha=1
if and only if
p
has a nonzero even-degree term,
in which case
2n
is the least even degree appearing in
p
,
and similarly the analogous criterion for
\upbeta=1
and least odd degree term in
p
.

Proof.

First note that

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx,y^{2}+\updelta_{x}p)\kern-2.5pt).

We exhibit an automorphism of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
that takes the two generators
of the Jacobian ideal to
(xy+yx)\text{(unit)}
and

\left(y^{2}+\upbeta x^{2m}+\upalpha x^{2n-1}\right)\text{(unit)}
, respectively,
where either
\upalpha=0
or
2n\geq 4
is the least even degree appearing in
p
,
and either
\upbeta=0
or
2m+1\geq 5
is the least odd degree appearing in
p
.
This proves all the claims.

Parsing
\updelta_{x}p
into even and odd terms, write the Jacobi algebra relations as

xy+yx\quad\text{and}\quad y^{2}+ax^{2N}u+bx^{2M-1}v

where
u,v\in\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
are each either a unit or zero,
N,M\geq 2
,
and
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
are any nonzero numbers that carry through the calculation
undisturbed; we choose
a=2N+1
and
b=2M
at the end.

Suppose in the first place that
u\neq 0
, then fix a square root
s=\sqrt{u}\in\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
and consider the unitriangular automorphism
\upvarphi
sending
x\mapsto x
,
y\mapsto ys
. By
6.8
this induces a topological isomorphism

\bar{\upvarphi}\colon\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)}.

In the codomain of this map,
y
commutes with
x^{2}
and thus commutes with
s\in\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
. It follows that
\bar{\upvarphi}(y^{2})=ysys=y^{2}s^{2}=yu
and thus

\bar{\upvarphi}(y^{2}+\updelta_{x}p)=(y^{2}+ax^{2N})u+bx^{2M-1}v.

By
2.11
(
1
)(
3
) after right multiplying by the unit
u^{-1}
, we obtain an isomorphism

\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx,y^{2}+\updelta_{x}p)\kern-2.5pt)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx,y^{2}+ax^{2N}+bx^{2M-1}\tfrac{v}{u})\kern-2.5pt)}.

(6.H)

If
v=0
, then (
6.H
) asserts that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2N+1})
, and so we are done. Hence we may assume that
v\neq 0
.

As
u
and
v
are both unit power series in
x^{2}
, so is
\frac{v}{u}
.
By
6.13
(
2
), since
2N-2M+1
is nonzero, we may choose a unit

t\in\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
such that
t^{2N}=t^{2M-1}v(xt)/u(xt)
.
Consider the unitriangular automorphism
\uppsi
sending
x\mapsto xt
,
y\mapsto yt^{N}
. Again by
6.8
there is an induced topological isomorphism

\bar{\uppsi}\colon\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)}.

Clearly
x
commutes with
t,t^{N}\in\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
, and further in the codomain of
\bar{\uppsi}
the element
y
commutes with
x^{2}
and thus commutes with
t,t^{N}\in\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
. Thus

\displaystyle\bar{\uppsi}(y^{2}+ax^{2N}+bx^{2M-1}\tfrac{v}{u})

\displaystyle=y^{2}t^{2N}+ax^{2N}t^{2N}+bx^{2M-1}t^{2M-1}u(xt)/v(xt)

\displaystyle=(y^{2}+ax^{2N}+bx^{2M-1})t^{2N}.

Again
2.11
(
1
)(
3
) then induces an isomorphism

\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx,y^{2}+ax^{2N}+bx^{2M-1}\tfrac{v}{u})\kern-2.5pt)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(xy+yx,y^{2}+ax^{2N}+bx^{2M-1})\kern-2.5pt)}.

(6.I)

Setting
a=2N+1
and
b=2M
, the right hand side is
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2N+1}+x^{2M})
. Hence composing (
6.H
) with (
6.I
) gives an isomorphism
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2N+1}+x^{2M})
.

The case
u=0
and
v\neq 0
works in exactly the same way as the case
u\neq 0
and
v=0
above, applying an automorphism with
s=\sqrt{v}
, while the case
u=v=0
is trivial.
∎

The above is not quite yet in normal form, since some of the polynomial potentials in
6.14
have isomorphic Jacobi algebras. The next step is to discard cases where the odd term in
p(x)
has significantly greater degree than the even term.

Lemma 6.15
.

If
f=xy^{2}+x^{2n}+\upvarepsilon x^{2m+1}
where
\upvarepsilon\in\{0,1\}
and
m\geq n
, then
y^{3}\in(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)
. In particular
y^{3}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
.

Proof.

Set
I=(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)=(y^{2}+2nx^{2n-1}+\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{2m},xy+yx)
, and below write
\equiv
for an equality mod
I
. Since
y^{2}\equiv-2nx^{2n-1}-\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{2m}
, multiplying on the left by
y
and on the right by
y
gives

\displaystyle-2nx^{2n-1}y-\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{2m}y\equiv y^{3}

\displaystyle\equiv-2nyx^{2n-1}-\upvarepsilon(2m+1)yx^{2m}

\displaystyle\equiv 2nx^{2n-1}y-\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{2m}y

where the last line holds since
xy\equiv-yx
using the second generator of
I
. Inspecting the right and lefthand sides, the
x^{2m}y
terms cancel, and so
4nx^{2n-1}y\equiv 0
, thus
x^{2n-1}y\equiv 0
. Finally, since
m\geq n
, taking out the common factor we see that

y^{3}\equiv(2n-\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{2m-2n-1})x^{2n-1}y\equiv 0.

Thus
y^{3}\in I
. The final statement follows immediately.
∎

Corollary 6.16
.

If
f=xy^{2}+x^{2n}+\upvarepsilon x^{2m+1}
where
m\geq 2n-1
, then
x^{4n-2}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
.

Proof.

Continue to write
\equiv
for an equality mod
(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)
. Then

\displaystyle x^{4n-2}=(-x^{2n-1})^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{1}{(2n)^{2}}(y^{2}+\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{2m})^{2}

(since
\updelta_{x}f\equiv 0
)

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{\upvarepsilon(2m+1)}{(2n)^{2}}(y^{2}x^{2m}+x^{2m}y^{2}+\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{4m})

(
y^{3}\equiv 0
by
6.15
)

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{\upvarepsilon(2m+1)}{(2n)^{2}}(2x^{2m}y^{2}+\upvarepsilon(2m+1)x^{4m}).

(
xy\equiv-yx
)

Taking out the
x^{2m}
common factor from the front, we may write
x^{4n-2}\equiv x^{2m}g
for some
g
with no constant term. Then, since
2m\geq 4n-2
by assumption, we see that
x^{4n-2}\equiv x^{4n-2}(x^{2m-(4n-2)}g)
, and so
x^{4n-2}(1-x^{2m-(4n-2)}g)\equiv 0
.

Given this statement holds mod
(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)
, it also holds mod
(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
, and hence
x^{4n-2}(1-x^{2m-(4n-2)}g)=0
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
. But there,
1-x^{2m-(4n-2)}g
is a unit, and so it follows that
x^{4n-2}=0
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, as required.
∎

The above two results combine to remove the case when the odd-degree
x
term is sufficiently larger than the even-degree
x
term, as follows.

Corollary 6.17
.

If
f=xy^{2}+x^{2n}+x^{2m+1}
where
m\geq 2n-1
, then
f\cong xy^{2}+x^{2n}
.

Proof.

By
6.16
we have
x^{4n-2}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
and
x^{4n-2}\in(\kern-2.5pt(y^{2}+2nx^{2n-1},xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt)
. Since
2m\geq 4n-2
, it follows that
x^{2m}
belongs to
both
of the ideals above, and thus

\displaystyle(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)

\displaystyle=(\kern-2.5pt(y^{2}+2nx^{2n-1}+(2m+1)x^{2m},xy+yx,x^{2m})\kern-2.5pt)

\displaystyle=(\kern-2.5pt(y^{2}+2nx^{2n-1},xy+yx,x^{2m})\kern-2.5pt)

\displaystyle=(\kern-2.5pt(y^{2}+2nx^{2n-1},xy+yx)\kern-2.5pt).

As this final ideal is obtained from
xy^{2}+x^{2n}
by differentiation, the result follows.
∎

Summarising the above gives the following, which is the main result of this subsection.

Corollary 6.18
.

Suppose that
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
where
(f_{3})^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has two roots. Then either

f\cong\begin{cases}xy^{2}&\\
xy^{2}+x^{2m+1}&m\geq 2\\
xy^{2}+x^{2n}&n\geq 2\\
xy^{2}+x^{2m+1}+x^{2n}&2\leq m\leq n-1\\
xy^{2}+x^{2n}+x^{2m+1}&2\leq n\leq m\leq 2(n-1)\end{cases}

All of the above are mutually non-isomorphic.

Proof.

The fact that the stated list covers all cases follows from
6.14
, using
6.17
to discount the case when the odd-degree
x
term is sufficiently larger than the even-degree
x
term. We now claim that the potentials listed give pairwise non-isomorphic Jacobi algebras.

The first two families both have infinite dimensional Jacobi algebras, whereas the bottom three are all finite dimensional. As such, the only possibilities for isomorphisms are between members in families one and two, or between members in families three, four and five. But
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2})^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}=\infty
, whereas
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2m+1})^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}=2m+2
, and so all members of families one and two are mutually non-isomorphic.

For the final three families, all members of families three and four and mutually non-isomorphic, as can be seen by extending the method of
[
BW1
, 4.7]
, or by using
[
Ka
, 5.10]
directly. Further, all members of family five are also mutually non-isomorphic for dimension reasons, since for
f
in family five
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}=2m+2
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=(2m+2)+4(n-1)
by either
[
vG
, §5]
or §
4.3
, and thus we can distinguish between all different
m
and
n
. The only remaining possibility is an isomorphism between a member of family five, and a member of family three or four. But by above the dimension of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for
f
in family five is even, and the dimension of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)
for
g
in families three and four is odd
[
Ka
, 5.10]
, so there can be no such isomorphisms.
∎

6.4.
Overview of Type D normal forms

The previous subsections combine to give the following, which is the main result of this section.

Theorem 6.19
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)=2
and
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=2
. Then either

f\cong\begin{cases}z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+z_{d-2}^{2}+xy^{2}&\\
z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+z_{d-2}^{2}+xy^{2}+x^{2m+1}&\mbox{with }m\geq 1\\
z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+z_{d-2}^{2}+xy^{2}+x^{2n}&\mbox{with }n\geq 2\\
z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+z_{d-2}^{2}+xy^{2}+x^{2n}+x^{2m+1}&\mbox{with }n\geq 2,\,\,n\leq m\leq 2n-2\\
z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+z_{d-2}^{2}+xy^{2}+x^{2m+1}+x^{2n}&\mbox{with }m\geq 1,\,\,n\geq m+1.\end{cases}

The Jacobi algebras of these potentials are all mutually non-isomorphic, and furthermore the following statements hold.

(1)

Every
f
in the first two families satisfies
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=1
.

(2)

Every
f
in the last three families satisfies
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=0
.

(a)

For any fixed
n\geq 2
, the algebras in families three and four combine to give
n-1
non-isomorphic Jacobi algebras, all of which satisfy
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}=2n+1
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=(2n+1)+4(n-1)=6n-3
.

(b)

In the fifth family,
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}=2m+2
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=(2m+2)+4(n-1)
.

Proof.

By the Splitting Lemma
4.5
the condition
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)=2
implies that
f\cong z_{1}^{2}+\ldots+z_{d-2}^{2}+g
for some
g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
. The condition
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=2
is then equivalent to the first two cases in
4.13
, namely those
g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
with
g_{3}\neq 0
for which
g_{3}^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
has either two or three distinct linear factors. The options for all such
g
thus follow from combining
6.7
and
6.18

The fact that
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{3})=1
follows since
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{3})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{3}+y^{3})
by linear change in coordinates, and
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{3}+y^{3})=1
by
6.5
(
1
). The statements that
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2m-1})=1
for all
m\geq 2
and
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2})=1
can be shown by a very similar explicit method as in the proof of
6.5
(
1
), or alternatively by using
8.5
below, once we know (in
8.16
) that all such Jacobi algebras are contraction algebras. The stated vector space dimensions of the Jacobi algebras in all remaining cases have already been justified in the proofs of
6.7
and
6.18
respectively.

The fact that the above are all mutually non-isomorphic, and thus a list of normal forms, then follows. Indeed, by inspecting
\mathfrak{J}
-dimension, the only possible isomorphisms are between members of families one and two, or between members of families three, four and five. Given we have just added the normal forms of
6.7
to the normal forms of
6.18
, the only remaining possible isomorphisms are between these two cases. But again, either the dimension of the abelianisation, or the dimension of the contraction algebra itself, distinguishes in all cases.
∎

7.
Central Elements and General Elephants

This section algebraically extracts ADE information from the normal forms in §
1.2
, using generic central elements and contracted preprojective algebras.

7.1.
The Six Algebras

As notation, consider the following ADE Dynkin diagrams, which we also furnish with the information of their highest roots.
