\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

(7.A)

To each such Dynkin diagram, there is an associated preprojective algebra
\Uppi
(see e.g.
[
CBH
]
), which is a finite dimensional algebra. The vertices of the Dynkin diagram give rise to idempotents in the corresponding
\Uppi
. In each diagram in (
7.A
), let
e
be the idempotent corresponding to the unique vertex marked
, except for
E_{8}
when there are two cases:
e
is either the left
or the right
. From this information, consider the algebra
e\Uppi e
.

Remark 7.1
.

Before relating the above to the introduction, we remark that the fifth algebra in (
1.C
) has full presentation

\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle x,y,z\rangle}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x^{2}+y+z,\\
x^{4},\,\,z^{2}+xyx,\,\,yxy+y^{2}x+yx^{3}\end{array}\right)}.

(7.B)

There are many equivalent presentations, with the point being that all are necessarily less pretty than the other five in (
1.C
). Indeed,
7.2
below proves that the algebra in (
7.B
) is isomorphic to
e\Uppi e
, where
e
is the vertex
marked
5
in
E_{8}
. This is the only time that the chosen vertex
in (
7.A
) is not the central vertex in the Dynkin diagram, and so slightly different behaviour should be expected. We also remark that this algebra is strictly needed in order for noncommutative singularity theory to distinguish between the two different types of
E_{8}
flop (of length
5
and
6
, respectively).

The upshot from (
7.A
) is that there are six algebras
e\Uppi e
, corresponding to the six different vertices marked
. The following result asserts that these give a presentation-free description of the six algebras of (
1.C
) in the introduction.

Lemma 7.2
.

Consider in order the algebras
e\Uppi e
where
e
is the vertex
with label
1,\ldots,6
in (
7.A
). This list is isomorphic to the list of algebras in (
1.C
), reading left to right.

Proof.

When
is a central vertex (which covers all cases except the awkward one in
7.1
), the isomorphism is a direct application of
[
M2
, Theorem 1]
; see also
[
CB
, p53]
. The final remaining case when
e
is the vertex
marked
5
in
E_{8}
, can be proved using Auslander–Reiten theory; alternatively we may simply observe that
e\Uppi e
is the factor of the algebra in
[
K
, 1.3(5)]
by the ideal generated (in the notation there) by
a,a^{*},t,T_{0}^{\upbeta},\ldots,T_{1}^{\updelta}
. The result
[
K
, 1.3(5)]
then gives a presentation

e\Uppi e\cong\,\,\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\upbeta,\upgamma\rangle}{(\upbeta^{4},\,\,\upgamma\upbeta\upgamma+\upgamma^{2}\upbeta+\upgamma\upbeta^{3},\,\,(\upgamma+\upbeta^{2})^{2}+\upbeta\upgamma\upbeta)}

which is clearly isomorphic to the algebra in (
7.B
).
∎

7.2.
Generic Central Sections

For any
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
, set
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f))
to be the centre of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, and write
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
for the Jacobson radical of
\mathcal{Z}
. Recall that
\mathfrak{J}
denotes the Jacobson radical of the local ring
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
.

Lemma 7.3
.

We have
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathfrak{J}\cap\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}\cong\mathbb{C}
. Thus
\mathcal{Z}
is also a local ring.

Proof.

Set
J_{f}=(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{1}f,\ldots,\updelta_{d}f)\kern-2.5pt)
, then it is clear that
\mathfrak{J}\cap\mathcal{Z}=\{g+J_{f}\in\mathcal{Z}\mid g\in\mathfrak{n}\}
. This set is clearly a two-sided ideal of
\mathcal{Z}
, and further
1+\mathfrak{J}\cap\mathcal{Z}
consists of units in
\mathcal{Z}
. These two properties imply that
\mathfrak{J}\cap\mathcal{Z}
equals the Jacobson radical
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
of
\mathcal{Z}
, see e.g.
[
L1
, 4.5]
. The fact that
\mathcal{Z}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}\cong\mathbb{C}
is clear.
∎

Generic elements of the centre
\mathcal{Z}
will be used to intrinsically extract ADE information.

Definition 7.4
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
, we say that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
has Type
X
if for all finite dimensional vector spaces
V\subset\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
such that
V\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
, there exists a Zariski open subset
U
of
V
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(u)\cong e\Uppi e
for all
u\in U
, where
\Uppi
is the preprojective algebra of Type
X
, and
e
is an idempotent marked
in (
7.A
).

Equivalently, in the language of
[
R1
, 2.5]
,
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
has Type
X
provided that a general hyperplane section
u
of
\mathcal{Z}
satisfies
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(u)\cong e\Uppi e
where
\Uppi
is the preprojective algebra of Type
X
, and
e
is an idempotent marked
in (
7.A
). We also remark that there are two different Type
E_{8}
’s in
7.4
, corresponding to the two different choices of
in
E_{8}
in (
7.A
). This feature matches the two different
E_{8}
cases in the length classification of flops
[
KM
]
.

Much like the definition of cDV singularities,
7.4
is only designed to be useful in specific situations. Indeed, for general
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
, it is not clear whether the centre
\mathcal{Z}
of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is noetherian, nor whether its maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is finitely generated as a
\mathcal{Z}
-module. Consequently, work is required to establish that
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
is finite dimensional, which is needed for there to exist a finite dimensional vector space
V
surjecting onto it.

When
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite dimensional, these difficulties disappear, since
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
and thus
\mathcal{Z}
,
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
are all finite dimensional vector spaces. Other cases are more tricky, but for our purposes the following suffices.

Lemma 7.5
.

If
f
is a normal form from
6.19
, then the following statements hold.

(1)

If
u\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, then
u\equiv\uplambda x^{2}+h
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
h\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
.

(2)

If further
f
has Type
D_{\infty,\infty}
or
D_{\infty,m}
, then
\mathcal{Z}\cong\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
.

In particular, in all cases
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2})<\infty
.

Proof.

(1) In all cases
\updelta_{y}f=xy+yx
, and so certainly
x^{2}
commutes with
y
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
. Obviously
x^{2}
commutes with
x
, thus since we are considering closed ideals, it follows that
x^{2}
is central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
. Similarly
y^{2}
is central.

We next claim that there are no elements in
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
that contain linear terms. Write
u\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
as
u=k+g
for some
k=\uplambda_{1}x+\uplambda_{2}y
and
g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
. Now
u
remains central after factoring by
\mathfrak{J}^{3}
, so set
I=(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,\mathfrak{n}^{3})\kern-2.5pt)
, and observe that

0+I=[x+I,u+I]=2\uplambda_{2}xy+I

For any (finite-dimensional)
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,\mathfrak{n}^{r})\kern-2.5pt)
,
r\geq 3
,
we may choose a basis of the form

1,x,\ldots,x^{i},xy,x^{2}y,\ldots,x^{j}y
for some
i,j\geq 2
.
Since
xy
forms part of this basis it follows that
\uplambda_{2}=0
. Repeating using the commutator
[y+I,u+I]
shows that
\uplambda_{1}=0
.

Thus
u=g
for some
g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
. Using the relation
xy+yx
to move
x
’s to the left, and the other relation to move
y^{2}
either to zero, to
x^{2}
, or into higher degree, write

u\equiv\uplambda_{1}x^{2}+\uplambda_{2}xy+h

in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, for some
h\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
. We next claim that
\uplambda_{2}=0
. Since
u
is central, and
x^{2}
is central, it follows that
v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\uplambda_{2}xy+h
is also central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
. In particular it is still central after factoring by
\mathfrak{J}^{4}
. Set
I=(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,\mathfrak{n}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
, so that

0+I=[x+I,v+I]=2\uplambda_{2}x^{2}y+I

and thus
2\uplambda_{2}x^{2}y\in I
. But
x^{2}y
forms part of a basis of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/I
, so
\uplambda_{2}=0
.

(2) Either set
I=(xy+yx,y^{2})
, or
(xy+yx,y^{2}+x^{2m})
, so by assumption
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/(\kern-2.5pt(I)\kern-2.5pt)
. Consider an arbitrary element
u\in\mathcal{Z}
. By using the first relation to move all the
x
’s to the left, and the second relation to either move
y^{2}
to zero or to higher powers of
x
, we may write
u\equiv p+qy
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, where by (
1
)
p\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]_{\geq 2}
and
q\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]_{\geq 2}
. Observe that in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)

0\equiv[x,u]\equiv[x,p+qy]=2xqy

and so
xqy\in(\kern-2.5pt(I)\kern-2.5pt)
. Thus
xq_{\leq t-3}y\in I+\mathfrak{n}^{t}
for all
t\geq 3
. Now
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/(I+\mathfrak{n}^{t})
has basis
1,x,\ldots,x^{t-1},y,xy,\ldots,x^{t-2}y
. Write
q_{\leq t-3}=\sum_{i=2}^{t-3}\uplambda_{i}x^{i}
, then the second part of this basis on the equation
xq_{\leq t-3}y\in I+\mathfrak{n}^{t}
shows that
\uplambda_{2}=\ldots=\uplambda_{t-3}=0
. This holds for all
t
, and so
q=0
.

Thus the central element
u\equiv p
. Splitting into even and odd terms, write
u\equiv P(x^{2})+xQ(x^{2})
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
P,Q\in\mathbb{C}[\![x]\!]_{\geq 1}
. Then, in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, since
x^{2}
is central

0\equiv[y,u]\equiv[y,P+xQ]\equiv-2(xQ)y.

Using the same argument as above,
Q=0
, and so
u\equiv P(x^{2})
, as claimed. This shows that
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{C}[\![x^{2}]\!]
, but since
x^{2}
is central by (
1
), equality holds, proving (
2
).

For the very last statement, all the finite-dimensional
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
satisfy
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2})<\infty
. Since the only other potentials in
6.19
are those in (
2
), where visibly
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2})=1
, the final statement follows.
∎

It follows from
7.5
that
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
is finite dimensional for any
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
with
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}(f)=2
and
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{rk}_{2}(f)=2
. The is a rather remarkable use of normal forms: we have no method to prove such a result without using
6.19
.

7.3.
ADE preliminaries

The next problem is to exhibit a single element of the centre
\mathcal{Z}
that gives an ADE quotient. For Type
A
and
D
this turns out to be easy, but Type
E
requires the following preparation.
Consider the elements

g_{6,n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}x^{2}+xyx+yx^{2}&\mbox{if }n=3t+1\mbox{ with }t\geq 1\\
x^{2}+xyx+yx^{2}+(-1)^{t}3^{t}(3t+2)x^{2t+1}&\mbox{if }n=3t+2\mbox{ with }t\geq 1\\
x^{2}+xyx+yx^{2}+(-1)^{t+1}3^{t}t(xyx^{2t-2}+yx^{2t-1})&\mbox{if }n=3t\mbox{ with }t\geq 2.\\
\end{cases}

The following establishes, in the cases
E_{6,n}
, that the centre of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is non-trivial, and that
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is at least two dimensional as a vector space.

Lemma 7.6
.

If
f
has Type
E_{6,n}
, then
x^{2}
is central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, as is
g_{6,n}
.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the relation
3x^{2}+y^{3}\equiv 0
, which implies that
yx^{2}\equiv-\tfrac{1}{3}y^{4}\equiv x^{2}y
and thus
x^{2}
commutes with
y
. Since
x^{2}
clearly commutes with
x
and we are considering closed ideals, it follows that
x^{2}
is central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
.

For the second statement, we establish the first case, with the proofs of all other cases being similar. For this, it suffices to show that
xyx+yx^{2}
is also central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
when
n=3t+1
with
t\geq 1
. We first claim that
xyxy-yxyx\in(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
. This follows since
n-1=3t
, then
y^{n-1}\equiv(-3x^{2})^{t}
is central, and thus the commutator

(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)\ni[x,\updelta_{y}f]\equiv(xyxy-yxyx)+n(xy^{n-1}-y^{n-1}x)\equiv xyxy-yxyx.

Using this, again with the fact that
x^{2}
is central, it follows that

\displaystyle[x,xyx+yx^{2}]

\displaystyle\equiv(yx^{3}+x^{3}y)-(x^{3}y+yx^{3})=0

\displaystyle[y,xyx+yx^{2}]

\displaystyle\equiv(yxyx+y^{2}x^{2})-(xyxy+yx^{2}y)\equiv 0.

Thus
xyx+yx^{2}
commutes with both
x
and
y
, and so is central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
.
∎

7.4.
Extracting ADE

We are now in a position to extract ADE using general hyperplane sections of the centre. In what follows, in the case that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite dimensional, all ideals are automatically closed. In the cases when
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=1
this fact is also true for Type
A
by inspection, and for Type
D
by e.g.
8.4
and
8.17
below. As such, in the following technically we should temporarily write
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(\kern-2.5pt(g)\kern-2.5pt)
when considering Type
D_{\infty,m}
and
D_{\infty,\infty}
until we have established
8.4
and
8.17
. However, since
8.4
and
8.17
are logically independent of what follows, we refrain from doing so, and drop the double bracket to ease notation.

Theorem 7.7
.

Consider the normal forms
A_{n}
,
D_{n,m}
,
D_{n,\infty}
,
E_{6,n}
,
A_{\infty}
,
D_{\infty,m}
,
D_{\infty,\infty}
and
E_{6,\infty}
from §
1.2
. In each case, define an element
s
as follows

Type

Normal form

Conditions

s

A

z_{1}^{2}+\ldots+z_{d-2}^{2}+x^{2}+\upvarepsilon_{1}y^{n}

n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}\cup\{\infty\}

y

D

z_{1}^{2}+\ldots+z_{d-2}^{2}+xy^{2}+\upvarepsilon_{2}x^{2n}+\upvarepsilon_{3}x^{2m-1}

m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}\cup\{\infty\}

x^{2}

E

z_{1}^{2}+\ldots+z_{d-2}^{2}+x^{3}+xy^{3}+\upvarepsilon_{4}y^{n}

n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 4}

g_{6,n}

where
g_{6,n}
is defined in §
7.3
above. Then the following statements hold.

(1)

The element
s
is central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, and
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(s)\cong e\Uppi e
, where
\Uppi
is the preprojective algebra of Type
A_{1}
,
D_{4}
, or
E_{6}
, and
e
is the idempotent marked
.

(2)

Normal forms of Type
A
and
D
give rise to Jacobi algebras which have Type
A
and
D
respectively, in the sense of
7.4
.

Proof.

For (1), Type
A
is clear, since
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]/y^{n-1}
or
\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]
depending on whether
\upvarepsilon_{1}
is
1
or
0
. In both cases
y
is central, and the quotient is
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/y\cong\mathbb{C}\cong e\Uppi e
where
\Uppi
is the preprojective algebra of Type
A_{1}
.

Type
D
is similar. The fact
x^{2}
is central was justified in
7.5
(
1
). But then since
(x^{2})
is a closed ideal of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, setting
\uplambda_{2}=(2n)\upvarepsilon_{2}
and
\uplambda_{3}=(2m-1)\upvarepsilon_{3}
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(x^{2})

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},xy+yx,y^{2}+\uplambda_{2}x^{2n-1}+\uplambda_{3}x^{2m-2})\kern-2.5pt)}

(by
2.11
)

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},xy+yx,y^{2})\kern-2.5pt)}

(since
2n-1\geq 2
and
2m-2\geq 2
)

\displaystyle\cong e\Uppi e

(by
7.2
)

where
\Uppi
is the preprojective algebra of Type
D_{4}
, and
e
is the central vertex.

Type
E
is more involved. All proofs turn out to be similar, so here we illustrate the technique by considering the case
f
of Type
E_{6,n}
with
n=3t+1
and
t\geq 2
. Certainly
g_{6,n}
is central by
7.6
. After rescaling the
x
and
y
appropriately,

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(-x^{2}+y^{3},xy^{2}+yxy+y^{2}x+y^{3t})\kern-2.5pt)}\,\,\reflectbox{$\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}$}\,\,\Upgamma

and we work on the right-hand side. Under this identification, the element
g_{6,n}
becomes
\uplambda x^{2}+\upmu(xyx+yx^{2})
for some non-zero scalars
\uplambda
and
\upmu
.
We now claim that for any non-zero scalars
\uplambda
and
\upmu
the factor

A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\Upgamma}{(\uplambda x^{2}+\upmu(xyx+yx^{2}))}=\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(-x^{2}+y^{3},xy^{2}+yxy+y^{2}x+y^{3t},\uplambda x^{2}+\upmu(xyx+yx^{2}))\kern-2.5pt)}

is isomorphic to the model algebra
B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},y^{3},(x+y)^{3})\kern-2.5pt)\cong e\Uppi e
in
7.2
. The result will then follow, since for particular
\uplambda,\upmu
, there is an isomorphism
A\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(g_{6,n})
.

To establish the claim, note first that
x^{3}\equiv 0
in
A
for any
t\geq 2
, as follows. The additional relation gives
-\uplambda x^{3}\equiv\upmu(x^{2}yx+xyx^{2})
, which equals
\upmu(yx^{3}+x^{3}y)
since
x^{2}
is central. Repeating, we may push
x^{3}
into higher and higher degrees, and so
x^{3}
belongs to the closed ideal defining
A
, as claimed. Given
x^{3}\equiv 0
in
A
, it follows from the first relation that
y^{6}\equiv x^{4}\equiv 0
in
A
, and since
t\geq 2
also that
y^{3t}\equiv 0
in
A
. Consequently

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(-x^{2}+y^{3},xy^{2}+yxy+y^{2}x,\uplambda x^{2}+\upmu(xyx+yx^{2}),y^{3t})\kern-2.5pt)}

(7.C)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(-x^{2}+y^{3},xy^{2}+yxy+y^{2}x,\uplambda x^{2}+\upmu(xyx+yx^{2}))\kern-2.5pt)}.

(7.D)

where the last equality holds since
y^{6}
belongs to the closed ideal in (
7.C
), and
t\geq 2
. This latter presentation has no dependence on
t
.

Now, composing the automorphism
\upphi\colon\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
defined by

x\mapsto x-(xy+yx)+yxy,\qquad y\mapsto(x+2y)-y^{2}-yx

with
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\twoheadrightarrow B
gives a surjective homomorphism
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\twoheadrightarrow B
. It is elementary to check that the three relations of
A
in (
7.D
) map to zero, and hence since
\upphi
is continuous, it extends to the closure of ideals and thus induces a surjection
\upphi\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
.

Using the same method, it is also elementary to check that the automorphism
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
given by

x\mapsto-\tfrac{2}{3}x(3-2x)+(y-2)xy,\qquad y\mapsto x(1+\tfrac{25}{48}x)+y(1+\tfrac{1}{4}y+x)

descends to a surjective map
B\twoheadrightarrow A
. Thus
\dim A\leq\dim B=12
, and in particular
\dim A
is also finite. The previous surjection
A\twoheadrightarrow B
then implies that
\dim A=\dim B
, so that
\upphi\colon A\to B
, being a surjective map between algebras of the same dimension, is necessarily an isomorphism.

(2) For potentials of Type
A
, clearly
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is either
\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]/(y^{n-1})
or
\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]
, both of which are commutative, so
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
and further
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
is spanned by the image of
y
. Given any finite dimensional vector space
V\subset\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
such that
\uppi\colon V\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
, set
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\{\uplambda\in\mathbb{A}^{1}\mid\uplambda\neq 0\}
,
\mathcal{U}=\uppi^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{1})
, and let
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Then for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
, write
u=\uplambda y+p
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
\uplambda\neq 0
and some
p\in\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]_{\geq 2}
. In particular
u
equals
y
multiplied by a unit, and so
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(u)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(y)\cong\mathbb{C}\cong e\Uppi e
, where
\Uppi
is the preprojective algebra of Type
A
.

Lastly, consider Type
D
. By
7.5
all potentials
f
in
6.19
satisfy
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2})<\infty
. Hence we can again consider a finite dimensional vector space
V\subset\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, such that
\uppi\colon V\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
. Since
x^{2}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
by (
1
), and
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}
contains no linear terms (as justified in
7.5
(
1
)),
x^{2}
is non-zero in
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\phantom{2}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
. Thus set
b_{1}=x^{2}+\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}
, and extend to a basis
b_{1},\ldots,b_{t}
. Set
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\{\sum\uplambda_{i}b_{i}\mid\uplambda_{1}\neq 0\}
, and
\mathcal{U}=\uppi^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{1})
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
, then by
7.5
(
1
)
u\equiv\uplambda x^{2}+h
in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, for some
h\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
. The assumption
u\in\mathcal{U}
ensures that
\uplambda\neq 0
. By the relation
xy\equiv-yx
, we may pull all the
x
’s in
h
to the left, and since
h
has order at least three, afterwards each term either starts with
x^{2}
, or ends with
y^{2}
. Thus in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)

h\equiv x^{2}r+xy^{2}p+y^{2}q

for some
r\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 1}
,
q\in\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]_{\geq 1}
and
p\in\mathbb{C}[\![y]\!]
. Consequently
u\equiv x^{2}(\uplambda+r)+xy^{2}p+y^{2}q
,
and further using the relation
\updelta_{x}f
, it follows that

u\equiv x^{2}\big(\,\uplambda+r-(\uplambda_{2}x^{2n-2}+\uplambda_{3}x^{2m-3})p-(\uplambda_{2}x^{2n-3}+\uplambda_{3}x^{2m-4})q\,\big)

where again
\uplambda_{2}=(2n)\upvarepsilon_{2}
and
\uplambda_{3}=(2m-1)\upvarepsilon_{3}
. The term in brackets is a unit: since
n,m\geq 2
its only degree zero term is
\uplambda
, which by assumption is non-zero. It follows that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(u)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(x^{2})
, and so the result follows by (
1
).
∎

Remark 7.8
.

The need for taking a generic, or at least well chosen, central element in
7.7
is essential. Indeed, by
7.6
, for
f
of Type
E_{6,n}
the element
x^{2}
is central in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
. However

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(x^{2})\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},y^{3},(x+y)^{3}-xyx)\kern-2.5pt)}\ncong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},y^{3},(x+y)^{3})\kern-2.5pt)}=e\Uppi e

(7.E)

even although both sides have dimension twelve. Write
\mathcal{U}
for the open set given by the non-vanishing of the co-efficient of
x^{2}
, then (
7.E
) together with
7.7
(
1
) assert that the isomorphism class of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(u)
is not constant along
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Consequently, for Type
E
a smaller generic open set is required.

Remark 7.9
.

In the proof of
7.7
(
1
) above, the inverse of
\upphi\colon A\to B
is not the constructed map
B\twoheadrightarrow A
, rather
\upphi^{-1}
is induced by the much more non-obvious automorphism

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto x+\tfrac{1}{2}(xy+yx)-\tfrac{1}{8}yxy

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto\tfrac{1}{2}(-x+y)+\tfrac{1}{8}(x^{2}-3xy-yx+y^{2})+{}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\tfrac{1}{64}(5yx^{2}+12yxy+16y^{2}x)+\tfrac{3}{64}y^{2}xy+\tfrac{7}{128}y^{2}x^{2}.

8.
Geometric Corollaries

The previous results have geometric consequences. Section
8.1
classifies contraction algebras, up to isomorphism, from all Type
A
and
D
flopping contractions. This immediately gives, in §
8.2
, a classification of Type
A
and
D
flops, and it also has consequences to GV invariants. Then §
8.3
constructs the first, and conjecturally only, infinite family of Type
D
divisor-to-curve contractions. Using this, and known results from flops, we then prove that the Realisation Conjecture (
1.11
) is true, except possibly for some exceptional cases, establishing
1.12
in the introduction. The last subsection classifies contraction algebras that can arise from Type
A
and Type
D_{4}
divisor-to-curve contractions.

8.1.
Classification of contraction algebras for A and D flops

In this section we classify contraction algebras that can arise from Type
A
and
D
flops, in both cases without referring to any classification of such flops (noting that a Type
D
flop classification arises as a consequence, in §
8.2
). We will show that the only possible options are those finite dimensional Jacobi algebras in
5.1
and
6.19
respectively.
We use the notation of §
1.5
freely; in particular,

\mathcal{R}
is commutative noetherian and in applications
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
is the base of a simple 3-fold flop.

Remark 8.1
.

In both
8.2
and
8.9
below we classify the contraction algebras within a given type, but in fact more is true. By
[
HT
, 4.6]
if
\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{con}}
is the contraction algebra of a Type
X
flopping contraction which is isomorphic to a contraction algebra
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
of a Type
Y
flopping contraction, then
X=Y
. Hence, the algebras in
8.2
below are the contraction algebras of all possible Type
A
flops, and only of Type
A
flops, and the algebras in
8.9
are the contraction algebras of all possible Type
D
flops, and only of Type
D
flops.

The classification in Type
A
is elementary.

Proposition 8.2
.

If
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is a contraction algebra of a Type
A
flopping contraction, then
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2}+y^{n})
for some
n\geq 2
. Furthermore, any other contraction algebra of any other type cannot be isomorphic to such a Jacobi algebra.

Proof.

Consider
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
from an arbitrary Type
A
flop
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
. By a now standard argument of Van den Bergh
[
V2
, A.1]
, any indecomposable CM
\mathcal{R}
-module necessarily has rank one. Further, since
\mathcal{R}
is normal the endomorphism ring of any rank one CM module is isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}
. Hence
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
, being defined to be a factor of
\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits_{\mathcal{R}}(N)
for some indecomposable CM
\mathcal{R}
-module
N
, is thus a factor of
\mathcal{R}
, and hence is commutative.

Since
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
f
, combining
5.1
and
5.4
we see that
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2}+y^{n})
or
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2})
. The last case is impossible, since
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}<\infty
given that
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
is a flop
[
DW3
]
.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

A more geometric proof of
8.2

uses Reid’s Pagoda classification of Type
A
flops
[
R2
]
and then applies
[
DW1
, 3.10]
to conclude that
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2}+y^{n})
for some
n
.

Type
D
is much more involved, and requires multiple preliminary results. In the following, by an
\mathcal{R}
-algebra
\Upgamma
we simply mean that there exists a homomorphism
\mathcal{R}\to Z(\Upgamma)
, where
Z(\Upgamma)
is the centre. From
3.2
, let
\mathfrak{J}(\Upgamma)
denote the Jacobson radical of
\Upgamma
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\Upgamma
be an
\mathcal{R}
-algebra, where
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{m})
is commutative local noetherian, and suppose that
\Upgamma
is finitely generated as an
\mathcal{R}
-module.

(1)

The
\mathfrak{J}(\Upgamma)
-adic topology coincides with the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology on
\Upgamma
.

(2)

Every ideal of
\Upgamma
is closed with respect to the
\mathfrak{J}(\Upgamma)
-adic and the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topologies.

(3)

If
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{m})
is complete local, then
\Upgamma
is complete with respect to both the
\mathfrak{J}(\Upgamma)
-adic and the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topologies.

Proof.

(1) Since
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{m})
is local, and
\Upgamma
is finitely generated as an
\mathcal{R}
-module, it follows immediately from e.g.
[
L1
, 20.6]
that there exists
n\geq 1
such that

\mathfrak{J}(\Upgamma)^{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}\Upgamma\subseteq\mathfrak{J}(\Upgamma).

(8.A)

From this, it is clear that the
\mathfrak{J}(\Upgamma)
-adic and the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topologies coincide.

(2) We show that for any finitely generated
\mathcal{R}
-module
M
, with submodule
N
, then
N
is closed in
M
. Given this, applying (
1
) to
M=\Upgamma
and
N=I
proves the result. But since
M
is finitely generated, and
\mathcal{R}
is noetherian, Krull’s intersection theorem
[
M1
, 8.10(1)]
immediately shows that
M/N
is separated, and hence
N
is closed in
M
.

(3) Again, this is well known. Since
\Upgamma
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{R}
-module, and
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{m})
is complete local and noetherian, it follows from e.g.
[
L1
, 21.33]
that
\Upgamma
is
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete, and hence the result follows by (
1
).
∎

Corollary 8.5
.

If
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is a contraction algebra associated to a crepant
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
as above, then
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}=0,1
. Furthermore, the following statements hold.

(1)

\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}=0
if and only if
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
is a flop.

(2)

\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}=1
if and only if
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
is a divisorial contraction to a curve.

Proof.

\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is module finite over
\mathcal{R}
, being a factor of an NCCR
[
DW1
]
.

Now if
M
is any finitely generated
\mathcal{R}
-module (e.g.
M=\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
), since
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{m})
is local
\dim_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
can be defined using the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology, as the growth rate of the function
\mathrm{length}(M/\mathfrak{m}^{i}M)
, see e.g.
[
E1
, §12.1]
. Taking suitable powers of the inclusions in (
8.A
), it is elementary to see that the two sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{r\in\mathbb{R}\mid\textrm{for some $c\in\mathbb{R}$, }\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}/\mathfrak{J}^{n})\leq cn^{r}\text{ for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{r\in\mathbb{R}\mid\textrm{for some $c\in\mathbb{R}$, }\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}/\mathfrak{m}^{n}\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}})\leq cn^{r}\text{ for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\right\}

are equal, so
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}=\mathrm{inf}\,\mathcal{S}_{1}=\mathrm{inf}\,\mathcal{S}_{2}=\dim_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}})
.

The main result of
[
DW2
]
shows that
\mathop{\rm Supp}\nolimits_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}})
equals the contracted locus in
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
. Hence
\dim_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}})
is either
0
for flops, or
1
for divisor-to-curves respectively. It follows that
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}})
is either 0 or 1, respectively.
∎

Theorem 8.6
.

If
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
and
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is complete in its
\mathfrak{J}
-adic topology, then every central element
g+(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)\in\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
which is not a unit satisfies
g\in\mathfrak{n}^{2}
.

Proof.

Set
R=\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, and
\mathfrak{J}=\mathfrak{J}(R)
. Note that
I=(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta f)\kern-2.5pt)\subseteq\mathfrak{n}^{2}
, hence by
3.2
(
1
)

\mathfrak{J}^{2}=(\mathfrak{n}^{2}+I)/I=\mathfrak{n}^{2}/I.

(8.B)

Consider the central element
g^{\prime}=g+I
in
R
. Since
g^{\prime}
is not a unit in
R
, certainly
g^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{J}
. Further
g
cannot be a unit in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
, or it would descend to a unit, so
g\in\mathfrak{n}
.

We now suppose that
g\notin\mathfrak{n}^{2}
, and aim for a contradiction. Since
I\subseteq\mathfrak{n}^{2}
we see that
g^{\prime}=g+I\notin\mathfrak{J}^{2}
, and hence
0\neq g^{\prime}+\mathfrak{J}^{2}\in\mathfrak{J}/\mathfrak{J}^{2}
. But by (
8.B
) we have

\frac{\mathfrak{J}}{\mathfrak{J}^{2}}=\frac{\mathfrak{n}/I}{\mathfrak{n}^{2}/I}\cong\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathfrak{n}^{2}}

and so
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{J}/\mathfrak{J}^{2}=2
. Pick
0\neq h+\mathfrak{J}^{2}
to complete
g^{\prime}+\mathfrak{J}^{2}
to a basis of
\mathfrak{J}/\mathfrak{J}^{2}
.

But now since by assumption
R
is complete, and local, we may use
[
BIRS
, 3.1]
to present
R
. Consider the two-loop quiver
Q
, and map the trivial path to the identity of
R
, one of the loops
\ell_{1}
to
h
and the other loop
\ell_{2}
to
g^{\prime}
. Then by
[
BIRS
, 3.1]
the completeness of
R
extends this to a surjective homomorphism

\upvarphi\colon\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\twoheadrightarrow R

and the kernel is a closed ideal. Since the kernel contains the relation
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}-\ell_{2}\ell_{1}
, given
g^{\prime}
is central and so commutes with
h
, it follows that
(\kern-2.5pt(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}-\ell_{2}\ell_{1})\kern-2.5pt)\subseteq\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits\upvarphi
. In particular
\upvarphi
induces a surjection
\mathbb{C}[\![\ell_{1},\ell_{2}]\!]\twoheadrightarrow R
, and so
R
is commutative. Given this would contradict
5.4
, we conclude that
g\in\mathfrak{n}^{2}
.
∎

We will also require the following fact.

Proposition 8.7
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is the contraction algebra associated to a
D_{4}
contraction, then there is a central element
g\in\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
such that

\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}/(g)\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle x,y\rangle}{(x^{2},xy+yx,y^{2})}.

Proof.

Consider the 3-fold contraction
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
, and for generic
g\in\mathcal{R}
consider the pullback diagram

\mathcal{Y}
\mathcal{X}
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}/g
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}

By assumption,
\mathcal{R}/g
is a
D_{4}
Kleinian singularity. Now let
\mathrm{A}=\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits_{\mathcal{R}}(N)
be the NCCR associated to
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
, and view
g\in\mathcal{R}=Z(\mathrm{A})\subset\mathrm{A}
. Since
g
is generic, we can find such a
g
which is not contained in any associated prime ideal of
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathcal{R}}(N,N)
, from which
[
IW2
, Proof of 5.24]
(the assumptions there are Type
A
, but the method is general) shows that there is an isomorphism

\mathrm{A}/g\cong\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits_{\mathcal{R}/g}(N/gN).

From this isomorphism
[
DW1
, (3.C)]
establishes that
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}/g
is isomorphic to the contraction algebra associated to the surfaces contraction
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}/g
. The fact that the surfaces contraction algebra for this particular
D_{4}
contraction is
\mathbb{C}\langle x,y\rangle/(x^{2},xy+yx,y^{2})
can be deduced from
[
DW1
, 8.7]
; see also
[
M2
]
.
∎

We obtain the following remarkable consequence.

Corollary 8.8
.

No
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
with
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 3}
such that either
f_{3}=0
or
f_{3}\cong x^{3}
, can arise as a contraction algebra of a
D_{4}
flop, or a
D_{4}
divisor-to-curve contraction.

Proof.

Given such an
f
, suppose that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
for a contraction algebra of a
D_{4}
flop, or a
D_{4}
divisor-to-curve contraction
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
. Since
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is module finite over
\mathcal{R}
, being a factor of a NCCR
[
DW1
]
,
8.4
shows that
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
and hence
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is complete with respect to its radical-adic topology, and further every ideal is closed.

Further, by
8.7
we can find a central
g
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/(g)=4
, and since
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is complete, we can use
8.6
to write
g=g^{\prime}+(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f)\kern-2.5pt)
where
g^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{n}^{2}
. But since all ideals in
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
are closed, it follows that

\frac{\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)}{(g)}=\frac{\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)}{(\kern-2.5pt(g)\kern-2.5pt)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\ref{closed ideals 101}}}{{\cong}}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(\updelta_{x}f,\updelta_{y}f,g^{\prime})\kern-2.5pt)}

has dimension four. We claim that this is impossible, by exhibiting a factor with higher dimension. Reusing the notation in
5.4
, write
\mathcal{M}_{3}
for the set of all noncommutative monomials of degree
3
, and then we will factor by
(\kern-2.5pt(\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)
. In the two cases
f_{3}=x^{3}
and
f_{3}=0
, the factors are, respectively

\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},g^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(g^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)}.

The right hand algebra surjects onto the left hand algebra, so it suffices to prove that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/(\kern-2.5pt(x^{2},g^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{3})\kern-2.5pt)>4
. But since
g^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{n}^{2}
by
8.6
, inside the ideal we can replace
g^{\prime}
by
\uplambda_{1}xy+\uplambda_{2}yx+\uplambda_{3}y^{2}
, which gives at most one linear relation between
xy
,
yx
and
y^{2}
. From this, the statement is clear.
∎

The above gives rise to the following, which is the main result in this subsection.

Corollary 8.9
.

If
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is a contraction algebra of a Type
D
flopping contraction, then
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2}y+x^{2n})
for some
n\geq 2
, or
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2n}+x^{2m-1})
for some
m,n\geq 2
with
m\leq 2n-1
.

Proof.

Consider
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
from an arbitrary Type
D
flop. By
[
KM
]
necessarily the elephant is
D_{4}
, so
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is not commutative by
8.7
, since
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
has a factor which is not commutative. As
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
, appealing to
5.4
then gives
f_{2}=0
.

From this,
8.8
asserts that
f_{3}\neq 0
, and
f_{3}\ncong\ell^{3}
. Hence by
6.19

\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
f
in the list stated there. Only the bottom three families are possible, since
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}<\infty
given the contraction is a flop
[
DW3
]
.
∎

8.2.
Classification of Flops

The above results for contraction algebras classify flops, given that the Donovan–Wemyss conjecture is true
[
DW1
,
A3
,
JKM
]
.

Theorem 8.10
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

Type
A
flops are classified by Type
A
normal forms in Table
1
.

(2)

Type
D
flops are classified by Type
D
normal forms in Table
1
.

Furthermore, Type
E
flops are classified by Type
E
normal forms.

Proof.

The fact that the isomorphism class of the contraction algebra classifies flops is established in
[
JKM
]
. Thus (1) follows from the fact in
8.2
that the contraction algebras of Type
A
flops are precisely those Jacobi algebras coming from the Type
A
normal forms. Similarly, (2) follows from
8.9
and the fact that each normal form is realisable from geometry
[
vG
,
Ka
]
(see also
8.11
below). Since the remaining Type
E
normal forms cannot correspond to either Type
A
or Type
D
geometry (by either
8.2
or
8.8
), it follows that the contraction algebras of the remaining Type
E
flops must be isomorphic to Jacobi algebras of Type
E
normal forms. Note that the Type
E
normal forms stated in Table
1
are genuine examples; full details of others will appear elsewhere
[
BW2
]
.
∎

The classification of contraction algebras in
8.9
then has the following consequence.

Theorem 8.11
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between lattice points in the diagram in
1.6
and the base singularities
0\in\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
of Type
D
flops, given by

(n,m)\mapsto\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\left(\frac{\mathbb{C}[\![u,v,x,y]\!]}{u^{2}+v^{2}y-x(y^{2n+1}+(x+\upvarepsilon y^{m})^{2})}\right)

where
\upvarepsilon=1
if the lattice point is contained within the shaded region, and
\upvarepsilon=0
otherwise.

In particular, Type
D
flops do not admit moduli. Furthermore, the following hold.

(1)

The quasi-homogeneous Type D flops are precisely those outside the shaded region, and these are the standard Laufer family.

(2)

The
GV
invariants
n_{1},n_{2}
of the flopping contraction associated to a point
(n,m)
are written in green. The ovals group together flops with the same
GV
invariants.

Proof.

It is immediate from
8.10
that the Type
D
normal forms in Table
1
classify Type
D
flops. In particular, once we exhibit one flop for each Type
D
normal form in Table
1
, which has contraction algebra isomorphic to the prescribed Jacobi algebra, then the geometric classification is complete.

In the indexing of the diagram, for
(n,m)
with
n,m\geq 1
, consider the corresponding potential
xy^{2}+x^{2n+2}+x^{2m+1}
. Under this assignment, the points
(n,m)
in the shaded region correspond to the normal forms
D_{n+1,m+1}
, which by definition have a restriction on
m
relative to
n
. The lattice points not in the shaded region, namely those
(n,2n+1)
, correspond to
xy^{2}+x^{2n+2}+x^{4n+3}
. By
6.17
this is isomorphic to
xy^{2}+x^{2n+2}
, which is the normal form
D_{n+1,\infty}
in Table
1
. Thus, the lattice points stated are in bijection with the Type
D
normal forms.

Now by
[
vG
, §2.2]
and
[
Ka
]
, given any potential
xy^{2}+x^{2n+2}+x^{2m+1}
, the claimed commutative ring in the statement is the base of a flopping contraction, and further the corresponding contraction algebra is isomorphic to
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2n+2}+x^{2m+1})
. The first statement regarding the bijection follows.

The fact that the quasi-homogeneous singularities correspond to those outside the shaded region follows from
[
vG2
, §2.2.4]
, which computes the Milnor and Tjurina numbers. For the final statement regarding GV invariants, by
6.19
(
2
) and Toda’s dimension formula
[
T
, 1.1]
, we can read off the GV invariants. Indeed, by
[
T
]
the pair
n_{1},n_{2}
where
n_{1}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}
and
n_{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}(\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)^{\mathop{\rm ab}\nolimits}))
are precisely the GV invariants for length two flops. Only those pairs illustrated in the diagram in
1.6
(or Figure
4
) appear.
∎

Remark 8.12
.

The map in
8.11
is in fact well defined on all points
(n,m)
with
m,n\geq 1
, not just those marked in the picture in
1.6
. This follows since the commutative ring in
8.11
is always the base of a Type
D
flop, for every
(n,m)

[
vG
, §2.2]
. The point is that the domain needs to be restricted in order to obtain a bijection. The previous result
6.17
shows that any lattice point
(n,m)
with
m\geq 2n+1
gives an isomorphic flop to the lattice point
(n,2n+1)
.

Simply inspecting
8.11
(
2
) and the diagram in
1.6
gives the following corollary, which illustrates the significant gaps in the possible GV invariants that can arise.

Corollary 8.13
.

Consider
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Then
a,b
are the GV invariants for a Type
D
flopping contraction if and only if either

(1)

(a,b)=(2m+3,m)
for some
m\geq 1
, or

(2)

(a,b)=(2n,b)
for some
n\geq 2
, with
b\geq n-1
.

Further, when
a=2m+3
there are precisely
m+1
distinct contraction algebras realising
(a,b)
, up to isomorphism, whilst for any given
(2n,b)
the contraction algebra is unique.

Remark 8.14
.

It is possible to instead index the GV invariants to the classifying potentials, and this is done in Figure
4
below.

List of
p(x)

xy^{2}+p(x)
is one

associated to each
p(x)
describes the GV invariants of any

8.3.
Constructing divisor to curve contractions

In the list of potentials in
6.19
, the first appears as the contraction algebra of a divisor-to-curve contraction in
[
DW4
, 2.18]
. The second family, with
m=1
, is isomorphic to
x^{3}+y^{3}
, and so appears as a contraction algebra in
[
DW4
, 2.25]
. All the other three families are contraction algebras of
D_{4}
flops by
[
vG
,
Ka
]
, and the above subsection.

Motivated by Conjecture
1.11
, this subsection will fill the last remaining gap, and show that the whole of the second family in
6.19
, with arbitrary
m
, are realised as the contraction algebra of a divisor-to-curve contraction.

Remark 8.15
.

In the proof below we will first construct the contraction algebraically, before passing to the formal fibre to realise the contraction algebra. This algebraic construction is advantageous, since it conceptually distinguishes between the cases: in
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits R_{\infty}
below, which locally realises
D_{\infty,\infty}
, the origin is
cD_{4}
whilst all other points on the singular locus are
cA_{2}
. In contrast, in
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits R_{m}
below, which locally realises
D_{\infty,m}
, the origin is
cD_{4}
whilst all other points on the singular locus are
cA_{1}
. Compare the pictures in
[
DW4
, 2.18]
and
[
DW4
, 2.25]
, and also
[
W
]
.

Proposition 8.16
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, consider the element of
\mathbb{C}[\![X,Y,Z,T]\!]
defined by

F_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}Y(X^{m}+Y)^{2}+XZ^{2}-T^{2}&\mbox{if }m\geq 1\\
Y^{3}+XZ^{2}-T^{2}&\mbox{if }m=\infty\\
\end{array}\right.

and set
\mathcal{R}_{m}=\mathbb{C}[\![X,Y,Z,T]\!]/F_{m}
. Then the following statements hold.

(1)

\mathop{\rm Sing}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}_{m})^{\mathrm{red}}=(X^{m}+Y,Z,T)
if
m\geq 1
, and
(Y,Z,T)
if
m=\infty
.

(2)

In either case, blowing up this locus gives rise to a crepant Type
D
divisorial contraction to a curve
\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{m}
where
\mathcal{X}_{m}
is smooth.

(3)

The contraction algebra of
\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{m}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2m+1})
when
m\geq 1
, respectively
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2})
when
m=\infty
.

(4)

\mathcal{R}_{m}\cong\mathcal{R}_{n}
if and only if
m=n
, and so the
F_{m}
are all distinct up to isomorphism and so form an infinite family.

Proof.

(1) is immediate.

(2) Working first on the case of finite
m\geq 1
, consider the affine algebra

R_{m}=\frac{\mathbb{C}[r,s,u,v]}{u^{2}-r(r-s^{m})^{2}-sv^{2}}

whose completion at the origin is
\mathcal{R}_{m}
, in coordinates
(r,s,u,v)=(-Y,X,T,Z)
.
The blowup along
(u,v,r-s^{m})
is covered by two affine patches: the first is
U=\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathbb{C}[s,y_{0},y_{1}]
, with
y_{0}=u/(r-s^{m})
and
y_{1}=v/(r-s^{m})
, the second chart is a smooth hypersurface, and the map from
U
to the base is given by

\displaystyle(s,y_{0},y_{1})

\displaystyle\mapsto(y_{0}^{2}-sy_{1}^{2},\,s,\,y_{0}(y_{0}^{2}-sy_{1}^{2}-s^{m}),\,y_{1}(y_{0}^{2}-sy_{1}^{2}-s^{m})).

The exceptional locus in
U
is the divisor
y_{0}^{2}-sy_{1}^{2}-s^{m}=0
.
Pulling the canonical basis of differentials on
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits R_{m}
back to
U
gives

f_{2}^{*}\left(\frac{dr\wedge ds\wedge dv}{u}\right)=\frac{d(y_{0}^{2}-sy_{1}^{2})\wedge ds\wedge d(y_{1}(y_{0}^{2}-sy_{1}^{2}-s^{m}))}{y_{0}(y_{0}^{2}-sy_{1}^{2}-s^{m})}=2ds\wedge dy_{0}\wedge dy_{1}

which is a regular differential on
U
, and in particular has no zero or pole along the exceptional divisor.
Thus the map is crepant, as claimed. The case
m=\infty
is similar, but easier, as both open charts are affine
3
-space.

(3) The easiest way to establish the claim is to recognise
F_{m}
as a pullback from the universal
D_{4}
flop and apply restriction theorems for contraction algebras. Consider the six-dimensional universal
D_{4}
flop, given in
[
K
, (1.1)]
as

\mathcal{R}=\frac{\mathbb{C}[r,s,t,u,v,w,z]}{u^{2}-rw^{2}+2zvw-sv^{2}+(rs-z^{2})t^{2}}

and its universal family
\mathcal{Y}\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
, which is an isomorphism away from the singular locus in
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
. As observed by Van Garderen
[
vG2
, §2.2.3]
, slicing by the sequence
g_{1}=z
,
g_{2}=r-w-s^{m}
, and
g_{3}=t
yields a commutative diagram

Y=Y_{3}
Y_{2}
Y_{1}
\mathcal{Y}
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{3}
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{2}
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{1}
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}
\scriptstyle f

where
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}/g_{1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{R}_{1}/g_{2}
and
\mathcal{R}_{3}=\mathcal{R}_{2}/g_{3}
.
The result is the affine algebra

\mathcal{R}_{3}=\frac{\mathbb{C}[r,s,u,v]}{u^{2}-r(r-s^{m})^{2}-sv^{2}}

whose completion at the origin is
\mathcal{R}_{m}
. The pullback

f\colon Y\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{3}
is visibly an isomorphism away from
\mathop{\rm Sing}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\mathop{\rm red}\nolimits}=(u,v,r-s^{m})
,
so in particular is birational.

Van Garderen observes that
\mathcal{R}_{3}
is an integral domain
[
vG2
, 2.12]
and
Y
is smooth
[
vG2
, 2.13]
,
and that each
g_{i}
is a
slice
, in the terminology of
[
vG2
, 2.9]
, which
implies that
f
is projective and surjective with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}

[
vG2
, 2.10]
.

Furthermore, the tilting bundle yielding a derived equivalence between
\mathcal{Y}
and
\Lambda\in\CM\mathcal{R}
restricts to give a derived equivalence between
Y
and
\Lambda\otimes\mathcal{R}_{3}

[
vG2
, (2.11)]
. Since
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
is a regular sequence,
\Lambda\otimes\mathcal{R}_{3}\in\CM\mathcal{R}_{3}
and so in particular
f
is crepant
[
IW1
, 4.14]
. Since visibly both the blowup in (
2
) and
f
are crepant resolutions of the same variety, and both containing no flopping curves, they must be isomorphic (as varieties over the base
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits R_{m}
) since minimal models are unique up to flop. Thus the contraction algebra associated to (
2
) is isomorphic to the contraction algebra of the formal fibre of
f
. But by
[
vG2
, 2.8]
this is the claimed Jacobi algebra, namely
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2m+1})
when
m\geq 1
, respectively
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2})
when
m=\infty
.

(4) If
\mathcal{R}_{m}\cong\mathcal{R}_{n}
are isomorphic, then the contraction algebras of
\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{m}
and of
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathcal{R}_{n}
must be isomorphic. But then their abelianisations must also be isomorphic, and so in particular must have the same dimension. But the abelianisations have dimension
2(m+1)
and
2(n+1)
respectively, and hence
m=n
.
∎

The start of this subsection, combined with
8.16
, then gives the following.

Corollary 8.17
.

All the potentials in
6.19
are geometric.

In turn, this establishes
1.12
in the introduction.

Corollary 8.18
.

Conjecture
1.11
is true, except for the one remaining unresolved case when
f\cong x^{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4}
, where some further analysis is required.

Proof.

Every
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
. If
f_{2}\neq 0
the result is
5.2
, so we can assume
f_{2}=0
. We need
f_{3}\neq 0
so that
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\leq 1
, and
4.13
splits into three cases. The first two cases are covered by
6.19
, and
8.17
asserts that these are all geometric. The only remaining, unresolved, case from
4.13
is when
f_{3}\cong x^{3}
.
∎

Remark 8.19
.

It is possible to change variables to see that all type
D
normal forms can be realised in a uniform way. Indeed, the contraction algebra associated to the
cD_{4}
singularity

\frac{\mathbb{C}[\![u,v,x,y]\!]}{u^{2}+v^{2}y-x(\upvarepsilon_{2}y^{2n+1}-(x-\upvarepsilon_{3}y^{m})^{2})}

realises the general Type
D
potential
f=xy^{2}+\upvarepsilon_{2}x^{2n}+\upvarepsilon_{3}x^{2m-1}
, with the convention that each
\upvarepsilon_{i}
is either
0
or
1
.

Remark 8.20
.

Much like in Pagoda
[
R2
]
for Type
A
, it is also possible to view each Type
D
divisor-to-curve contraction as an infinite limit of flops. The Type
D
situation is more delicate, since there are more possible directions in which to take such a limit. In relation to the classification of Type
D
flops in
8.11
, the following are the limits which give rise to the divisor-to-curve contractions in
8.16
.
\scriptstyle F_{1}
\scriptstyle F_{2}
\scriptstyle F_{3}
\scriptstyle F_{4}
\scriptstyle F_{5}
\scriptstyle F_{6}
\scriptstyle F_{7}
\scriptstyle F_{\infty}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle\bullet
