\ldots
\vdots

Without the normal forms from noncommutative singularity theory, it is hard to either see or predict the above purely geometrically.

8.4.
Classification for A and D divisor-to-curve contractions

This section is the divisor-to-curve analogue of §
8.1
.

Proposition 8.21
.

If
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is a contraction algebra of a Type
A
divisor-to-curve contraction, then
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2})
.

Proof.

For the same homological reason as in
8.2
, in Type
A
the contraction algebra
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is necessarily commutative. Since the contraction is divisor-to-curve, necessarily
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}=\infty

[
DW3
]
. Combining
5.4
and
5.1
, we see that
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2})
, since
x^{2}
is the only infinite dimensional example in
5.1
.
∎

The following is the analogue of
8.9
. However, it is slightly weaker, due to two key geometric facts having only been developed in the flops setting: (1) Katz–Morrison
[
KM
]
asserts that Type
D
is generically Type
D_{4}
for flops, but this is open in the divisor-to-curve setting, and (2) Hua–Toda
[
HT
]
asserts that the isomorphism class of a contraction algebra determines the type, but again only for flops.

However, we can say the following, without using any geometric classifications.

Proposition 8.22
.

If
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is a contraction algebra of a Type
D_{4}
divisor-to-curve contraction, then
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(x^{2}y)
, or
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(xy^{2}+x^{2m+1})
for some
m\geq 2
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to
8.9
. The algebra
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
is not commutative by
8.7
, since
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}
has a factor which is not commutative. As
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle x,y\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq 2}
, appealing to
5.4
then gives
f_{2}=0
.

From this,
8.8
asserts that
f_{3}\neq 0
, and
f_{3}\ncong\ell^{3}
. Hence by
6.19

\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for some
f
in the list stated there. Only the top two families are possible, since
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{con}}=\infty
given the contraction is divisor-to-curve
[
DW3
]
.
∎

Appendix A
\mathfrak{J}
-dimension restrictions

The papers
[
ISm
,
IS2
]
introduce several new ideas that substantially strengthen the Golod–Shafarevich estimates
[
GS
]
for the growth of algebraic Jacobi rings, and prove that almost all have cubic or higher growth. In this appendix we extend the main results of
[
IS2
]
into the setting of formal noncommutative Jacobi algebras of
3.1
, in a manner which should be viewed as the analogue of Vinberg’s
[
V3
]
extension of Golod–Shafarevich into the setting of topological rings.

A.1.
Algebraic Notation

Throughout this appendix we let
d\geq 2
and consider
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle=\mathbb{C}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\rangle
. An element
F\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
is called a
superpotential
if it is cyclically symmetric, in the sense of
3.3
. For
m\geq k\geq 3
write

\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}=\{F\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\mid F\mbox{ is a superpotential with }F_{j}=0\mbox{ for }j<k\mbox{ and }j>m\},

where
F_{j}
is the homogeneous component of
F
of degree
j
, as in §
2.1
. In the special case
m=k
, write
\mathsf{SP}_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{SP}_{k,k}
, which consists of all homogeneous superpotentials of degree
k
, together with zero. Throughout, we will write elements of
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
and
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
by small letters
f
and
g
, and superpotentials by capital letters
F
,
G
.

With the (left) strike-off derivatives
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{i}
defined as in (
3.A
), the
algebraic Jacobi algebra
associated to a superpotential
F
is the algebra

\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\rangle}{(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}F,\ldots,\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}F)}=\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle}{I_{F}}

where
I_{F}=(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}F,\ldots\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}F)
is the two-sided ideal generated by
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}F,\ldots,\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}F
.
We write
\mathfrak{m}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\subset\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
, a maximal two-sided ideal,
and denote its image in
\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)
by
\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{m}/I_{F}
, the powers of which are

\mathfrak{R}^{i}=(\mathfrak{m}^{i}+I_{F})/I_{F}
.

The use of strike-off derivatives
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{i}
on superpotentials, as we use here to align
with the statements and results of
[
ISm
,
IS2
]
,
or cyclic derivatives
\updelta_{i}
on any potential, as in
3.1
, give equivalent theories but with minor differences in detail,
which we address in §
A.4
.

A.2.
Exact Potentials and Hilbert series

The differentiation package has two useful tools.
The first is the following Euler relation.

Lemma A.1
.

[
ISm
, 3.5]

If
F
is a superpotential, then
\sum_{i=1}^{d}[x_{i},\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{i}F]=0
.

The second is a sequence of right
\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)
-modules

0\to\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{d}_{3}}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)^{\oplus d}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{d}_{2}}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)^{\oplus d}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{d}_{1}}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{d}_{0}}\mathbb{C}\to 0

(A.A)

defined in e.g.
[
ISm
, 3.4]
. The precise form of the morphisms
\mathsf{d}_{i}
will not concern us, as below we will only require the following two facts.

(1)

[
ISm
, 3.6]
For any superpotential
F
, the sequence (
A.A
) is a complex, which is exact at the three right-most non-zero terms.

(2)

If further
F
is homogeneous, say
0\neq F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
, then the morphisms in the complex (
A.A
) satisfy
\deg(\mathsf{d}_{3})=1
,
\deg(\mathsf{d}_{2})=k-2
,
\deg(\mathsf{d}_{1})=1
, and
\deg(\mathsf{d}_{0})=0
.

Definition A.2
.

An element
F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
is called
exact
if (
A.A
) is exact.

If
G
is homogeneous, then the ideal
(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}G,\ldots,\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}G)
is a homogeneous ideal and so the graded decomposition of
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
induces a decomposition

\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)_{i}.

(A.B)

For
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
,
the boundary maps
\mathsf{d}_{i}
are homogeneous, and so furthermore the sequence (
A.A
)
also decomposes into graded pieces, or
homogeneous slices
, each of which is a complex of finite-dimensional vector spaces, exact at the codomains of the restrictions of
\mathsf{d}_{0}
,
\mathsf{d}_{1}
and
\mathsf{d}_{2}
.

Definition A.3
.

For
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
, (
A.B
) determines the
Hilbert series

of
\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)

\mathcal{H}_{G}=\sum_{i\geq 0}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)_{i}\big)t^{i}\in\mathbb{C}[\![t]\!].

Throughout, recall that
\mathsf{SP}_{k}
and
\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
are defined only when
k\geq 3
. The following is an easy consequence of the degree of the morphisms in (
2
) above, see e.g.
[
ISm
, §3]
.

Corollary A.4
.

If
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
is exact, then
\mathcal{H}_{G}=(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})^{-1}
.

It will be convenient to consider the following subset of homogeneous superpotentials

\mathsf{ESP}_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}\mid G\mbox{ is an exact potential, and }\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)\xrightarrow{x_{1}\cdot}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)\mbox{ is injective}\}.

Recall that
d\geq 2
is the number of variables in
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
.

Lemma A.5

(
[
IS2
, 2.1, 2.2]
)
.

If
k\geq 3
with
(d,k)\neq(2,3)
, then
\mathsf{ESP}_{k}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Set

g=\begin{cases}\sum_{\upsigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}}x_{d}^{k-d+1}x_{\upsigma(1)}\ldots x_{\upsigma(d-1)}&\mbox{if }k\geq d\\
x_{d}x_{d-1}\ldots x_{d-k+1}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}x_{j}x_{d}\mathsf{m}_{j}&\mbox{if }k<d\end{cases}

where
\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}
is the symmetric group,
\mathcal{S}=\{j\mid 1\leq j\leq d-1,\,j\neq d-k+1\}
, and the
\mathsf{m}_{j}
are explicit monomials explained in
[
IS2
, 2.2]
. It is a reasonably elementary calculation to show that
G=\sym(g)\in\mathsf{ESP}_{k}
; see
[
IS2
, 2.1, 2.2]
.
∎

It turns out, although we do not need this, that if
d=2
then
\mathsf{ESP}_{3}=\emptyset
. This is why the argument in
A.13
below fails in the
(d,k)=(2,3)
case.

Recalling that
\mathfrak{R}^{i}=(\mathfrak{m}^{i}+I_{F})/I_{F}
, the following is one of the main insights of
[
IS2
]
.

Corollary A.6
.

If
F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
with
F_{k}\in\mathsf{ESP}_{k}
, then left multiplication

\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)/\mathfrak{R}^{j}\xrightarrow{x_{1}\cdot}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)/\mathfrak{R}^{j+1}

is injective for all
j\geq 1
.

Proof.

This is
[
IS2
, 3.1]
. A proof in the notation used here is in
arXiv:2111.05900v1
, as Proposition A.12.
∎

A.3.
Very general elements

Fixing, once and for all, a basis
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{r}
of
\mathsf{SP}_{k}
,
we treat
\mathsf{SP}_{k}
as an irreducible algebraic family of superpotentials, and identify
it with
\mathbb{C}^{r}=\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}]
, where
\mathsf{t}=t_{1},\dots,t_{r}
are parameter variables
and any element of
\mathsf{SP}_{k}
is the specialisation
\mathsf{t}=a
at a point
a\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
of
the ‘generic’ superpotential
G_{\mathsf{t}}=\sum t_{i}f_{i}
.
In particular,
\mathsf{SP}_{k}
inherits the Zariski topology from
\mathbb{C}^{r}
.
Under this identification, it is natural to abbreviate
G_{a}\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
by
a\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
.

Lemma A.7

(c.f.
[
ISm
, 3.9]
)
.

Fix
(d,k)
and consider
\mathsf{SP}_{k}
with its Zariski topology as above.
For any
i\geq 0
,

(1)

there is a non-empty Zariski open subset
U_{i}\subset\mathsf{SP}_{k}

on which
\dim\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a})_{i}
is constant for all
a\in U_{i}
, and takes the minimum value of any
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
.

(2)

there is a largest Zariski open subset
V_{i}\subset U_{i}\cap U_{i+1}

on which the rank of the restriction

\mathsf{d}_{3}\colon\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a})_{i}\to(\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a})^{\oplus d})_{i+1}
for any
a\in V_{i}

is the maximum possible for a linear map between spaces of these dimensions
(i.e. is injective).

(3)

there is a largest Zariski open subset
W_{i}\subset V_{i}\cap U_{i+k-1}

on which the rank of the restriction
\mathsf{d}_{2}\colon(\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a})^{\oplus d})_{i+1}\to(\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a})^{\oplus d})_{i+k-1}

for any
a\in W_{i}
is the
maximum possible for a linear map whose kernel contains the image of
\mathsf{d}_{3}
.

Thus if
W_{i}
not empty, then for
a\in W_{i}
, the homogeneous slice of (
A.A
) with the domain of
\mathsf{d}_{3}
in degree
i

is an exact sequence of finite-dimensional vector spaces.

Proof.

(1) This is
[
IS1
, 2.2]
, proved as in
[
IS1
, 2.1]
.
The point is this: let
a_{0}\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
be a point at which the minimum dimension is achieved,
and choose a subset of the set
\Phi_{i}
of all monomials of degree
i

for which the (square) coefficient matrix of a basis of the kernel of
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle_{i}\to\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a_{0}})_{i}

with respect to
\Phi_{i}
is invertible (a section of the surjection). The entries of this square matrix are algebraic in
a
, so
it remains invertible as
a
varies in a Zariski open subset
a_{0}\in U_{i}\subset\mathsf{SP}_{k}
. Thus the kernel cannot get smaller, so must have constant dimension, and thus the minimum is also achieved for all
a\in U_{i}
.

(2) Within
U_{i}\cap U_{i+1}
, the map

\mathsf{d}_{3}\colon\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a})_{i}\to(\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G_{a})^{\oplus d})_{i+1}

is determined by a matrix of fixed size whose entries are functions in
a
.
Maximising its rank is an open condition on
a
, since it occurs on
the complement of a locus of vanishing minors.
(It is possible that all relevant minors vanish identically, in which case
the conclusion is simply that
V_{i}
is empty.)

(3) Similarly, maximising the rank of
\mathsf{d}_{2}
is an open condition prescribed by
minors that are functions in
a
. The condition that
the kernel contains the image of
\mathsf{d}_{3}
is already
imposed by the entries of the matrix, since
a\in V_{i}
and (
A.A
) is a complex.
∎

Corollary A.8

(
[
ISm
, 3.2]
)
.

Let
k\geq 3
and
(d,k)\neq(2,3)
.

(1)

For each
i\geq 0
, the minimum value achieved by
\dim\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)_{i}
for any
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
,
as in
A.7
(
1
)
, is the coefficient of
t^{i}
in the expansion of
(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})^{-1}
.

(2)

If
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
is exact, then
\mathcal{H}_{G}
attains the minimum coefficient
for every term
t^{i}
.

In particular, each
W_{i}
in
A.7
(
2
)
is non-empty,
and the minimum Hilbert series in the set
\left\{\mathcal{H}_{F}\mid F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}\right\}
is
(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})^{-1}
.

Proof.

The proof follows
[
ISm
, 3.2, 3.8]
, which is for
d=2
but generalises with no change,
using the existence of the exact potentials in
A.5
.
∎

For formal power series
\upvarphi(t)
and
\uppsi(t)
, write
\upvarphi\geq\uppsi
to mean that
the coefficients of
\upvarphi-\uppsi
are all non-negative.
If
\mathcal{P}
is a family of power series, then
\uppsi\in\mathcal{P}
is called the
minimum

if
\upvarphi\geq\uppsi
for all
\upvarphi\in\mathcal{P}
, noting that the minimum does not necessarily exist.

Proposition A.9
.

If
k\geq 3
and
(d,k)\neq(2,3)
, then there exists a countable intersection
\mathcal{U}
of non-empty Zariski opens of
\mathsf{SP}_{k}
such that
G\in\mathsf{ESP}_{k}
for all
G\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}
be the countable intersection over all
i\geq 0
of the Zariski open subsets
W_{i}
of
A.7
.
By
A.8
, each
W_{i}
is non-empty, thus this intersection is non-empty.
Similarly, there is another such countable intersection
\mathcal{U}_{2}\subset\mathsf{SP}_{k}

on which the left-multiplication map
x_{1}\colon\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)\to\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)

is injective, as injectivity maximises rank in each degree.
Since
x_{1}
is injective on the free algebra (at
\mathsf{t}=0
), or again applying

A.5
, this intersection is also non-empty.
Thus
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
is a non-empty countable intersection.
∎

For a given
F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
,
recall the notation
\mathfrak{m}
and
I_{F}
from §
A.1
.
The
Poincaré series
of
F
is defined to be

\mathcal{P}_{F}=\sum_{i\geq 0}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle}{\mathfrak{m}^{i+1}+I_{F}}\big)t^{i}.

(A.C)

This measures the growth of the quotients of
\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)
by the ideals
(\mathfrak{m}^{i+1}+I_{F})/I_{F}
.
The general situation is more delicate than the homogeneous case of
A.9
,
but still a minimum is achieved by a very general element.

Corollary A.10
.

If
k\geq 3
and
(d,k)\neq(2,3)
, then for any
m\geq k
there exists
F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
such that the following statements hold.

(1)

\mathcal{P}_{F}
is the minimum in
\{\mathcal{P}_{H}\mid H\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}\}
.

(2)

F_{\ord(F)}\in\mathcal{U}
, where
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathsf{SP}_{k}
is defined in
A.9
.

Proof.

Similar to
A.7
(
1
) (cf.
[
IS1
, 2.1]
),
minimising each coefficient of the Poincaré series is an open condition in a family, and so the
minimum is realised on
a countable intersection of non-empty Zariski open subsets
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
.

Consider the map
\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}\to\mathsf{SP}_{k}
given by
F\mapsto F_{k}
.
Intersecting the preimage of
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathsf{SP}_{k}
with
\mathcal{V}
determines a countable intersection of open subsets of

\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
on which the claims hold.
Since
\mathbb{C}
is uncountable, this set contains a closed point.
∎

A.4.
Power Series

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
, its Poincaré series is defined to be

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{f}=\sum(\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/\mathfrak{J}^{i})t^{i}

where recall that
\mathfrak{J}
is the Jacobson radical of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
.

When
F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
, we can view
F
as either a polynomial superpotential and form
\mathcal{P}_{F}
in (
A.C
), or we can view
F
as an element of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
and form
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{F}
. Since
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is defined with respect to the cyclic derivatives
\updelta
, and
\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is defined with respect to strike-off derivatives

6

, it is not quite true that
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{F}=\mathcal{P}_{F}
.

Lemma A.11
.

Given
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,m}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{G}=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{G}}
, where
\mathbb{G}=\sum_{i=k}^{m}\tfrac{1}{i}G_{i}
.
Thus, if
G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k}
, then
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{G}}=\mathcal{P}_{G}=\tfrac{1}{1-t}\mathcal{H}_{G}
where
\mathbb{G}=\tfrac{1}{k}G
.

Now for
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
and for any
i\geq 0
, set
\mathsf{f}_{i}=f_{\leq i}
and
\mathsf{F}_{i}=\sym(\mathsf{f}_{i})
. Then, continuing the notation of §
2.2
, and
recalling that
\mathfrak{R}^{i}=(\mathfrak{m}^{i}+I_{F})/I_{F}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/\mathfrak{J}^{i}

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\kern-2.5pt(\mathfrak{n}^{i},\updelta_{1}f,\ldots,\updelta_{d}f)\kern-2.5pt)}

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle}{(\mathfrak{m}^{i},\updelta_{1}\mathsf{f}_{i},\ldots,\updelta_{d}\mathsf{f}_{i})}

(A.D)

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle}{\big(\mathfrak{m}^{i},\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}(\sym\mathsf{f}_{i}),\ldots,\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}(\sym\mathsf{f}_{i})\big)}\cong\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{F}_{i})/\mathfrak{R}^{i}

This gives a term-by-term algebraicisation of the Poincaré series, by

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{f}=\sum_{i\geq 0}(\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{F}_{i})/\mathfrak{R}^{i})t^{i}.

A.5.
Main Result

The main result,
A.13
, requires the following elementary lemma.

Lemma A.12
.

For
d\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\geq 2
consider the formal power series

\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})}=\sum_{i\geq 0}b_{i}t^{i}.

Setting
b_{j}=0
for
j<0
, the following statements hold.

(1)

There is an equality
b_{j}=db_{j-1}-db_{j-k+1}+b_{j-k}+1
for all
j\geq 0
.

(2)

b_{0}=1
, and further
b_{j}=1+d+\ldots+d^{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq k-2
.

Proof.

(1) Treating the series as a sum over
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
b_{<0}=0
, and multiplying up shows
at once that
b_{0}=1
and that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}

b_{j}-(d+1)b_{j-1}+db_{j-2}+db_{j-k+1}-(d+1)b_{j-k}+b_{j-k-1}=0.

When
j=0
, the claimed equality in (
1
) holds.
For
j\geq 1
, splitting off a single
b_{j-1}
summand from the equation above we see by induction that

\displaystyle b_{j}

\displaystyle=db_{j-1}-db_{j-2}-db_{j-k+1}+(d+1)b_{j-k}-b_{j-k-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(db_{j-2}-db_{j-k}+b_{j-k-1}+1)

\displaystyle=db_{j-1}-db_{j-k+1}+b_{j-k}+1.

(2) For
1\leq j\leq k-2
, the equality in (
1
) reads
b_{j}=b_{j-1}d+1
, and so the result follows since
b_{0}=1
.
∎

The following is the main result of this appendix, and it asserts that, in almost all cases, the
\mathfrak{J}
-dimension of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is
\geq 3
. In particular, in almost all cases the Jacobi algebra is infinite dimensional, as a vector space. Recall from §
2.1
that
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq k}
consists of all those
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
for which
f_{j}=0
for all
j<k
, and note that
0\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq k}
.

Theorem A.13
.

Suppose that
d=2
and
k\geq 4
, or
d\geq 3
and
k\geq 3
. If
f\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
has order
k
, then
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\geq 3
.

The proof will show that the coefficients of the Poincaré series of
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)

are no smaller than those of

\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})}.

When
d=2
and
k=4
this lower bound is
1/\big((1-t)^{3}(1-t^{2})\big)
,
and when
d=k=3
it is
1/(1-t)^{4}
, both of which have polynomial growth of degree 3.
For all other
d,k
in the scope of the theorem, the growth is exponential
and
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=\infty
.

Proof.

Associated to the fixed
k=\ord(f)
and
d
is the power series

\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})}=\sum_{i\geq 0}b_{i}t^{i}.

The
b_{i}
s are integers that depend only on
k
and
d
. Similarly, associated to
k
and
d
are the positive integers
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots
defined to be

\displaystyle a_{j}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{min}\{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)}{\mathfrak{J}^{j+1}}\big)\mid g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle_{\geq k}\}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{min}\{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)}{\mathfrak{J}^{j+1}}\big)\mid g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle_{\geq k}\}

(truncate terms mod
\mathfrak{J}^{j+1}
)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{min}\{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)}{\mathfrak{J}^{j+1}}\big)\mid g\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle_{\geq k},\,g_{t}=0\mbox{ for }t>j+1\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{\mathfrak{n}^{j+1}}\big)&\mbox{if }j\leq k-2\\
\textnormal{min}\left\{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(G)}{\mathfrak{R}^{j+1}}\big)\mid G\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,j+1}\right\}&\mbox{if }j\geq k-1.\end{cases}.

(by (
A.4
))

Certainly
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{f}\geq\sum_{i\geq 0}a_{i}t^{i}
, by the minimality of the
a_{i}
. We claim that
a_{i}=b_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
, since then

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{f}\geq\sum_{i\geq 0}a_{i}t^{i}=\sum_{i\geq 0}b_{i}t^{i}=\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})},

which has the prescribed growth as in the statement of the result.

So, from here on we discard the original
f
, and instead prove that
a_{i}=b_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
. This is a statement which depends only on
k
and
d
.

Since by assumption
(d,k)\neq(2,3)
, by
A.5

\mathsf{ESP}_{k}\neq\emptyset
and so choose
G\in\mathsf{ESP}_{k}
. Since
G
is exact, by
A.4

\mathcal{H}_{G}=(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})^{-1}
, and so by
A.11
for
\mathbb{G}=\tfrac{1}{k}G
we have

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{G}}=\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{G}=\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-dt+dt^{k-1}-t^{k})}=\sum_{i\geq 0}b_{i}t^{i}.

Since
\mathbb{G}
exists, it follows immediately by minimality of the
a_{i}
s that
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
.

Now clearly
a_{0}=b_{0}=1
, and further for all
1\leq j\leq k-2
we have
a_{j}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{\mathfrak{n}^{j+1}}\big)
, which equals
b_{j}
by
A.12
(
2
). Further, since
d
relations of degree
k
can cut down the dimension of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}
by at most
d
, it follows that
a_{k-1}\geq 1+d+\ldots+d^{k}-d
. This equals
b_{k-1}
by
A.12
(
2
), and so
a_{k-1}\geq b_{k-1}
, which in turn forces
a_{k-1}=b_{k-1}
.

Thus, by induction we can suppose that
a_{j}=b_{j}
for all
0\leq j\leq s
, for some
s\geq k-1
. The proof will be completed once we show that
a_{s+1}=b_{s+1}
.

Now by
A.10
applied to
m=s+2
, there exists
F\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,s+2}
for which
\mathcal{P}_{F}
is the minimum in
\{\mathcal{P}_{H}\mid H\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,s+2}\}
, and further
F_{k}\in\mathcal{U}
where
\mathcal{U}
is from
A.9
. By the first of these facts, since for all
j\leq s+1
by truncation

a_{j}=\textnormal{min}\left\{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{H})}{\mathfrak{R}^{j+1}}\big)\mid\mathsf{H}\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,j+1}\right\}=\textnormal{min}\left\{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{H})}{\mathfrak{R}^{j+1}}\big)\mid\mathsf{H}\in\mathsf{SP}_{k,s+2}\right\}

it follows that

\mathcal{P}_{F}=\sum_{j=0}^{s+1}a_{j}t^{i}+\mathcal{O}_{s+2}.

(A.E)

On the other hand, since
F_{k}\in\mathcal{U}
, by definition
F_{k}\in\mathsf{ESP}_{k}
. Thus, by
A.6
for all
j\geq 1
the left multiplication by
x_{1}

\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)/\mathfrak{R}^{j}\xrightarrow{x_{1}\cdot}\mathrm{A}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(F)/\mathfrak{R}^{j+1}

is injective.

Set
I=(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}F,\ldots,\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}F)\subset\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
. The above asserts that there is an injection

\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle}{I+\mathfrak{m}^{j}}\xrightarrow{x_{1}\cdot}\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle}{I+\mathfrak{m}^{j+1}}

for all
j\geq 1
. This allows us to pick inductively sets of monomials
M_{j}
of
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
of degree
j
, starting with
M_{0}=\{1\}
, such that the following two conditions are satisfied.

(1)

There is an inclusion
x_{1}M_{j}\subseteq M_{j+1}
.

(2)

The necessarily disjoint union
N_{j}=M_{0}\cup\ldots\cup M_{j}
projects down to give a basis of
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle/(I+\mathfrak{m}^{j+1})
.

To fix notation, set
B_{j}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}(N_{j})\subset\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
, and define
\mathsf{b}_{j}
via the equality

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}B_{j}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\tfrac{\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle}{I+\mathfrak{m}^{j+1}}\big)=\mathsf{b}_{j}.

Note that the second equality implies that
\mathcal{P}_{F}=\sum_{i\geq 0}\mathsf{b}_{j}t^{j}
.

Write
V=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
,
R=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}F,\ldots,\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}F\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
, and for
j\geq 0
consider the quotient map
\uppi_{j}\colon\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle/\mathfrak{m}^{j+1}
.
By the definition of
\mathsf{b}_{j}
, for every
j\geq 0

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\uppi_{j}(I)=1+d+\ldots+d^{j}-\mathsf{b}_{j}.

(A.F)

We now apply the adapted Vinberg argument:
elements of the two-sided ideal
I
are sums of elements, each of which either starts
with an
x_{i}
or starts with a derivative
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{i}F
, so we can write

I=VI+R\,\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle.

Applying
\uppi_{j+1}
then gives an equality

\uppi_{j+1}(I)=\uppi_{j+1}(VI)+\uppi_{j+1}(R\,\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle).

Since
B_{j}
descends to span
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle/(I+\mathfrak{m}^{j+1})
, every element of
\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
may be written as an element in
B_{j}
, plus an element in
I
, plus an element in
\mathfrak{m}^{j+1}
. Projecting down this sum via
\uppi_{j+1}
, and noting that
RI\subset R\,\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
gets absorbed into
\uppi_{j+1}(I)
,
and elements of
R
have degree
\geq k
, it follows that mod
\mathfrak{m}^{j+1}
there is an equality

\uppi_{j+1}(I)=\uppi_{j+1}(VI)+\uppi_{j+1}(RB_{j+2-k}).

Write
R^{\prime}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{2}F,\ldots,\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{d}F\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\langle\mathsf{x}\rangle
, then using the Euler relation
\sum_{i=0}^{d}[x_{i},\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{i}F]=0
of
A.1
we may get rid of any appearance of the product
(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}F)x_{1}
at the cost of terms in the other summands. It follows that

\uppi_{j+1}(I)=\uppi_{j+1}(VI)+\uppi_{j+1}(R^{\prime}B_{j+2-k})+\uppi_{j+1}((\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{1}F)B^{+}_{j+2-k}),

where
B^{+}_{j+2-k}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{n\in N_{j+2-k}\mid n\neq x_{1}m\mbox{ for any }m\}
.

The proof is completed by estimating the dimension of each of the three individual summands.
Applying (
A.F
) for the first summand,

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\uppi_{j+1}(I)\leq d(1+d+\ldots+d^{j}-\mathsf{b}_{j})+(d-1)\mathsf{b}_{j+2-k}+(\mathsf{b}_{j+2-k}-\mathsf{b}_{j+1-k})

since
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}B^{+}_{j+2-k}\leq\mathsf{b}_{j+2-k}-\mathsf{b}_{j+1-k}

holds by construction of the
N_{j}
in (
1
).
Plugging (
A.F
) for
\uppi_{j+1}

into the above displayed equation, and then cancelling, it follows that

1-\mathsf{b}_{j+1}\leq-d\mathsf{b}_{j}+d\mathsf{b}_{j+2-k}-\mathsf{b}_{j+1-k}.

which after re-arranging gives

\mathsf{b}_{j+1}\geq\mathsf{b}_{j}d-\mathsf{b}_{j+2-k}d+\mathsf{b}_{j+1-k}+1.

(A.G)

Since
\mathcal{P}_{F}=\sum_{i\geq 0}\mathsf{b}_{j}t^{j}
, by (
A.E
) we see that
\mathsf{b}_{j}=a_{j}
for
0\leq j\leq s+1
, and hence

\displaystyle a_{s+1}

\displaystyle\geq a_{s}d-a_{s+2-k}d+a_{s+1-k}+1

((
A.G
) for
j=s
)

\displaystyle=b_{s}d-b_{s+2-k}d+b_{s+1-k}+1

(
a_{j}=b_{j}
for
j\leq s
by induction)

\displaystyle=b_{s+1}

(
A.12
(
1
) for
j=s+1
)

Since we already know
a_{s+1}\leq b_{s+1}
by minimality, the above forces
a_{s+1}=b_{s+1}
. Hence by induction
a_{j}=b_{j}
for all
j\geq 0
, and the result follows.
∎

Remark A.14
.

The above theorem establishes that often
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\geq 3
, whilst earlier sections considered the case
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\leq 1
. In general we do not know what, if anything, satisfies
1<\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)<3
, even in the case when
f
is a polynomial. Nor do we know whether
\mathop{\rm Jdim}\nolimits\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is always an integer.

A.6.
Contractibility Consequence

The following is an immediate geometric consequence of the above.

Theorem A.15
.

Let
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathcal{X}
be an irreducible rational curve in a smooth
CY

3
-fold, with
NC
deformation algebra
\Lambda_{\mathrm{def}}
, such that
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{C}|\mathcal{X}}\neq(-3,1)
. Then
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathcal{X}
contracts to a point suitably locally, without contracting a divisor, if and only if
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{def}}<\infty
.

Proof.

The case of
(-1,-1)
and
(-2,0)
-curves is of course well known
[
R2
,
DW1
]
. The point is that
(-4,2),(-5,3),\ldots
curves never contract
[
L2
, 4.1]
, and further as a consequence of
[
V1
]
their noncommutative deformations are given as the Jacobi algebra quotient of a free power series ring in
3,4,\ldots
variables. By

, these can never be finite dimensional. Thus in all these cases the statement is true, just since the curves never contract, and the deformation algebras are always infinite dimensional.
